THE SPECTRE OF DURANT

CASTLE.

MT name is Denis Brabazon, and I am, or rather was at the time of which I am about to write, a doctor, without any practice of my own or the means of purchasing one. Mv sister Dorothy and-y I lived in a big old house at Bydenham, not many. miuutes' walk from the Crystal Palace; the sa.id house together with a sum of two hundred pounds Oex annum having been left us by an old uncle whom we had never seen. Our Rtrents had died when we were both young, so that DoLothy and I were all in all to each other. Ever since I had passed my final exam. I had fretted and fumed at my forced inactivity, and scanned the medical journals and newspapers daily, vowing to take the lirst post as house or ship's surgou that I eculd obtain. It wanted but a few days to my twenty seventh birthday, when one morning I ,aw an ad. vertisement in the Standard, which seemed just the thing I had been looking for. A okillcd medi- cal man with a knowledge of mental cases, was required to take entire charge of A patient, and to reside in the house. Liberal remuneration offered. Now, as I had made insanity my special study, I certainly thought I was a fit and proper person to apply for the position, so I hurried off to an old friend of my father's who was, and is now,one of :he most eminent medical men in London. Ile kind!y wrote me out a strong recommendation, which I. enclosed with my application, and then await,ed with what patience I could command—which was very little—the reply which I felt almost certain I should receive.

The next few days we afterwards called " Dori: - thy's martyrdom,' for I was too excited to rest, and wandered in and out of the house with my big collie, "Tim," at my heels fifty or sixty times .5 day, and as it was the middle of winter, the amount of slush "Tim" and I m.4naged to deposit on the new carpets was something incredible. When I came down to breakfast on.the fourth morning after posting my application, I found Dorothy turning over and over a thick envelope with a crest on the flap, addressed to myself, and bearing a Welsh postmark wAch we found our- selves utterly unable to pronounce. Slipping her hand through my arm, we did our best to decip,ler the somewhat illiterate writing. The sheet of note paper was imposingly stamped "Dura,it Castle," and the letter appeared to be from the guardian of the patient. He stated in rather pompous phrase- ology, that my application backed by my friend's letter, bad received his careful consideration, and he had decided to engage me; that we should not dis- agree as to salary, and that he hoped to see me at Durant Castle in a week at the very latest. Then followed his signature—" Evan Jones." "Oh! Denny, .whatever shall I do without asked Dolly, rubbing her rough curly head on my shoulder, the tears sparkling in her dark eyes. "Why, you must have Etta down to stay with you whilst I am away," I answered, kissing her tenderly, for we nave a genuine affection for each other.

Etta was our cousin on our mother's side; a pretty golden-haired little fairy for whom I used to have a sneaking regard, which soon vanished when I found out what a thorough-paced little flirt she was.

We made a very poor breakfast that morning, Dolly and I. The coffee was allowed to grow cold, the toast and eggs ditto, whilst we talked of the coming separation. The next few days seemed to me a whirl of packing, friendly congratulations, and partings which I did not fully recover until nearing Pen-y-grantog in a train, which for slow- ness could not be matched even on the Continent.

Poor little Dolly! She had cried when we parted at Easton, and, regardless of grinning porters and idiotic masters, had clung around my neck in a way that made me feel horribly uncomfortable. "Oh, Denny, Denny," she whispered with a sob, "it will never be the same again. We shall never, never be the same to each other as we were before this parting." And I had kissed the pretty tear- stained face, and patted her encouragingly on the back until at last the train bore me away with my once immaculate collar and tie, over which I had spent so much time and patience that very morn- ing, rumpled in such an alarming manner that an old lady who was getting into the compartment at the first stopping-place took fright on catching a ght of it, and hurried off to another part of the train!

I laughed at the recollection in a half-hearted sort of way as I leaned back in the comfortable first class carriage, with my head buried in the blue tobacco-scented curtains, and my feet on the opposite cushions on which halt-a-dozen uncut papers were lying. Just as I was dropping off into a comfortable dose, worn out with the exertions of the last few days, a fiend, in green corduroy, yelled close to my ear, " Pawagrata, Pawagrata," and I started up to find that I was at my destination. The aforemen- tioned fiend seemed literally struck dumb with sur- prise when I alighted, and gazed open-mouthed at me as if I were some rara-aris, such as he had never seen in his sleepy "come day, go day, God- send-Sunday" existence. "Come, my man, see to my luggage, will you?" I cried sharply, at which he shambled off to the luggage-van, where he and the guard had a chat of quite tea minutes' duration, during which the engine-driver and stoker had stolidly disposed of a couple of large pies' which I imagine constituted their mid-day meal. When the train at last puffed and snorted itself out of sight, the solitary porter again approached, and asked if I was the gentleman for Durant Castle. I answered in the affirmative, on which he lugged my portmanteau and baggage to the end of the platform., through a gate, and deposited them on a small cart which stood outside. "How far is the Castle!" I asked. "Three miles, sir," said another official who had just come up, and who proved to be the station- master. _

"Three miles!" I echoed in dismay. "Yes air, I saw the carriage coming this way a few minutes ago. Perhaps it's corning to meet you, sir. Better wait. I did wait, and was rewarded by seeing the car- riage descending a. tortuous hill towards the station, and at last draw up in front of me with a flourish. "Are you Mr. Brabazon, sir!" said the coach- man, touching his hat. "I am," I answered. "All right, sir,"—holding open the carriage door —" Mr. Jones said you were to excuse his not coming to meet you, as he had business at the house- farm this afternoon."

I took my seat, and as I did so I heard the station- master say "How is Miss Durant to-day, Bond ? " and t•:e reply, "Worse," accompanied by a signifi- cant tapping of the cranium. Miss Durant! Was my patient a female? It seemed very much like it. and I fell to imagining an old maiden lady whose long residence in these wilds had driven her mad- Twenty minutes later, after driving up a tremendous hill, I could dis- tinctly hear the roar of the sea as it dashed upon the rock-bound coast, and leaniag out of the car- riage window I caught my first view of Durant Castle. Never, except in ruins on the banks of the Rhine, have I seen anything to compare with it for situation. Not that this was a ruin. On the con- trary, it frowned from the top of the hill in all its grandeur like some impregnable fortress. The light of the setting sun gleamed redly on the many win- dows, and the utter silence that reigned around, except for the ceaseless roar of the sea, caused an eerie sensation to creep over me. The immense gates rolled open on our approach, clanging to behind us, and as I alighted before a magnificent entrance, a thick-set man with sharp cunning eyes came down the steps with outstretched hand, and a smile on his lips. "You will excuse my not meeting you, Mr. Bra- bazon," he said politely. "I had an engagement which it was impossible to put off. Welcome to Durant Castle." I shook hands, with him, and noticed that two fingers were missing from the otherwise shapely hand laid on mine. "You' will no doubt wish for some refreshments, and to retire to your room so as to remove the traces of your journey," he added. "The housekeeper will show you the way. We dine at seven, and I then wish to have a conversation with you about your patient." Thanking him for his courtesy, I followed the housekeeper, a homely old lady, up the wide old oaken staircase ; "Tim," my collie, following closely at my heels.

The rooms allotted to me were cosy and cointort.. able, the windows commanding a splendid view of the wide-stretching sea. "1 hope you'll be comfortable, Mr. Brabazon," said Mrs. Bond, poking the large wood fire vigor- ously, and dusthig an imaginary speck of dirt from the polished tiles of the grate. "Thank you, I have no doubts on the subject," I replied pleasantly, I could see the good soul wanted to talk to me about my patient, so I ven- tured to ask, "How is Miss Durant to-day ?" "Poorly, only very poorly, dear lamb. She's just fading away. Ido hope you'll be able to do her good, doctor." "Well, I'll try, and that will show I'm willing, won't it?" I asked, as a light footfall resounded in the corridor. "Why, it do sound like Miss Lindsay a-coming here," replied the housekeeper. She had scarcely finished speaking, when the door was pushed open, and a girl with a slender willowly figure, clothed in white, a well-poised head, and a lovely profile-like--some exquisitely cut cameo— entered the room. The pretty violet eyes opened wide in surprise on seeing me, and "Tim," evi- dently liking the new corner, walked sedately, over to her, wagging his tail in a friendly manner, and pushed his cold black nose into her little hand. She patted the audacious carline on the head, pulled his long ears, and then rubbed his hair the wrong way, indignities which " Tim " bore with great good humour. Then she condescended to glance at me again. There was no madness expressed by those deep violet eyes—only a frightened, appeaiing, and at the same time, distrusttul look. "This is Mr. Brabazon," said Mrs. Bond, speak- ing to her young mistress gently and lovingly. Mr. Jones has engaged him as your doctor, Miss Lindsay." Once again the pathetic violet eyes seek my own, and I bow at the introduction.

"Indeed," is the only reply she vouchsafes as she stands pulling "Tim's" ears. "I hope we shall soon have you quite well again, Miss Durant," I said, with a kindly smile. She looked at me once again, and then with her hands tightly clasping "Tim's" massive silver collar, she asked in her low sweet voice— " Are you a friend of his V' " I felt slightly bewildered. "Of whose, Miss Durant?" "Evan Jones."

I saw that 1 must prevaricate. "I saw Mr. Jones for tne first time in my life juFt now, and of course politeness forbids my siying what impression I have received," I answered with another slight smile. Miss Durant regarded me wistfully for a few moments, and then moving nearer to me she whis- pered, with a frightened glance at the closed door, Will you keep it away ? ' I glanced at her sharply, bat the look in her violet eyes was most decidedly not madness only a great fear, which, if nob removed, might end in that awful malady. "Certainly, if it annoys you I will," I answered reassuringly. She looked so innocent, so lovely, and the violet eyes met my own so appealingly, that my pulses beat rather faster than usual, and a strange feeLng as yet took possession of me. "It is horrible, disgusting, loathsome," she said vehemently. "Four nights ago it appeared to me and wrote the dread message once again." "What dread message? Whatever do you mean, Miss Durant?" I asked wonderingly. "Do you not know ?'' regarding me wistfully. "A horrible headless spectre haunts Durant Castle, and has appeared to me in my bedroom several times. It writes in letters of fire on the wall, You Lindsay Durant, the last of the race, will most surely die February 21st, 1883.'"

I had detected the moment of entering Miss Lindsay's room a strong smell of phosphorous. The luminous writing on the wall could be produced by phosphorous. An idea that had first struck me a couple of weeks back, now assumed tangible pro- portions. Evan Jones had said to me. Miss Durant's birthday is on the t. 2nd of February. I suppose you, together with 1 :ie local medical man, will give a certificate of inst‘ •eity so that I may still manage the property for It( r until she recovers." And I had answered curtiy that, providing I was convinced of Miss Lindsay's mental capacity 1 should certainly do as he wished. • He was appar- ently satisfied, for he rubbed his hands together, and his cunning eyes glittered avariciously. "You knows Dr. Brabazon," he continued, "Miss Lindsay accepted me as her future husband some time ago, but since this distressing malady has occurred she has entirely forgotten the affair, and, in fact,acts as if she positively disliked me, although I can assure you such is not the case." "Indeed," I answered coldly. "Yes," he went on complacently, "and by the bye, Doctor, would you iike an instalment of your salary now, say fifty pounds," producing a pocket- book, and drawing out a bank-note for that amount. " I would rather wait until Miss Durant is fully cured, Mr. Jones, thank you,' I replied quickly, for I felt an inward repugnance to accepting money from this man.

This conversation recurred to me now as I sat over the glowing coals. Evan Jones was the only person who would benefit by Miss Lindsay's death before the 22nd of February. That he was unscru- pulous and selfish I was convincei ? Could he have anything to do with this spectre business. I crossed over to a cabinet which stood on the opposite side of the room, and unlocking a drawer drew from it a heavy Colts revolver. Carefully withdrawing the cartridges, I placed fresh ones in their places, and then, with a light laugh, put it in my breast pocket. Things would certainly be hot for Mr. Spectre if he appeared whilst I was in Miss Lind- say's room. How I loved her ! And she cared for me! Had I not read it in her eyes? For me—me, Dennis Brabazon ! What had I done to be so blessed?

It was the 21st of February, and the day accord- ing to the spectre, on which Lindsay had to die. It struck me very forcibly that the dying might be done, but not by Lindsay. For the last few nightP Mrs. Bond and I had sat in her room unknown to the rest of the household, and nothing had been seen or heard of the disturber of our peace. We generally sat behind the tapestry in one corner so that a look would show us the entire room, whilst we ourselves would be unseen.

Lindsay had avoided me since the night J had held her so closely in my arms, and, whenever I spoke to her, she blushed and faltered over her reply, to my infinite delight. I was waiting until after the 21st to declare my love, Sand although it was hard work, I struggled manfully to repress my feelings. Evan Jones, on his return, had sneered at the reported spectre, and tried to impress upon me that it proved Lindsay to be mad; but I was not to be impressed, and I think he grew suspicious, or he would never have done what he did. On the morning of the 21st he again started off on business, saying he should return in four or five days. The hours passed away very quietly, although I could see Lindsay was nervous and ill at ease— small wonder seeing that she had been warned it was her last day on earth. , When evening came round Ws. Bond and I took up our positions as usu,l, but not before I had spoken a few comforting words to Lindsay. The hours rolled slowly by. Mrs. Bond had quietly dropped off to sleep, wrapped up in shawls and rugs, until I could scarcely catch a glimpse of her face. Tne large silver lamp had been turned low, and Lindsay's regular breathing told that she was asleep. I peeped through the tapestry. The room was in semi-darkness, but I could see that it was perfectly empty except for our three selves,and as the door was fast locked and bolted, I knew no one could enter the room that way. I could just distinguish Lind- say's bright tumbled hair and sweet flushed face an it rested on the snow-white pillow, and then I leaned back in my chair and thought of how I would kiss and caress them on the morrow.

Downstairs in the hall a clock chimed the half- hour after midnight, and I had just made up my mind that nothing was going to happen, when a slight noise, so low as to be almost imperceptible, caused me to peep through the slit in the tapestry once again, and I saw a sight that froze the life- blood in my veins. Leaning over Miss Durant wa.s a loathisomc, putrifying, headless figure surrounded by a faint phosphorescent glow. A sickening odour began to fill the rom, making me gasp for breath. Then, suddenly, I saw a band upraised clutching a glitter- ing dagger, and from that hand two fingers were missing. I gripped my revolver quick as lightning, and raising it with an inarticulate cry of rage, I fired. A loud reverberating report rang out, followed by a shrill scream from Lindsay, who sat up in bed with her hair falling around her in wild disorder, and that painful look of horror in her eyes, whilst the figure realed several steps, and then fell with a dull thud on the floor. Mrs. Bond, terrified nearly out of her senses, sprang from her chair with another piercing scream. By this time I was at the bedside with my arms around Lindsay, soothing her with tender, loving words and passionate burning kisses, as she clung to me convulsively. The butler and several other menservants, followed by a crowd of frightened females, rushed into the room, and there on the floor was a dark red blot discolouring the carpet, and by its aide lay the spectre of Durant Castle. With angry threats the men tore away the flowing white garments in which it was wrapped and revealed the pale face of Evan Jones. "Why its Mr. Jones." they cried in surprise. Lindsay shuddered at the deathlike expression of the ghastly face. "He is not dead, is he, Dennis?" she asked tremblingly. "No, darling, only winged. I'll see to him," handing her over reluctantly to Mrs. Bond. I found on examination that I had only embedded a bullet in Jones' shoulder, and soon restored him to consciousness again. 'Hallo, so the game's up," be cried. " Nevsr mind, I've had my fling. Fifty thousand pounds of your money gone, Lindsay," he cried, with a hoarse laugh. "How did you get into the room?" I asked, sternly. "Secret door in that corner of the fire- place," was his reply, and on examination I found this to be true. Lindsay, on condition that Jones left England at once and for ever, promised not to prosecute him, and Jones, evidently surprised at this clemency, gladly jumped at the offer. I saw him safely on board an Australian-bound steamer, and just as they were on the point of starting, I pressed into his hand a bank note for one hundred pounds which Linn say had given me for him. "She hopes you fAll try to lead a better, nobler life now, Jones," I said, as I got on the tender which was to convey me ashore. The tears slowly gathered in his eyes, as he ' whispered huskily, "God bless her. She's an angel of goodness, and that was the last I saw of Evan Jones. Three days later, as I stood in the drawing-room at the Castle waiting for the dogcart to come to convey me to the station, (for I was once more returningto Sydenham,) the door opened, and Miss Lindsay came in, a slight flush on her cheeks. "Oh, I oeg your pardon, Dr. Brabazon, I thought Mrs. Bond was here," she faultered, retreating with the evident intention of avoiding me; but I was too quick for her, and standing with my back against the door, faced the pretty little rebel— "Lindsay," I said softly, "I am going away. What have you to say to me?" The violet eyes were shyly downcast, and the rich colour flooded her face. "Come sweetheart," said I, gently, holding out my arms. She raised her face then, with such a glory of love shining from her dear eyes, that I uttered a low cry of delight as I folded her closely to my throbbing hekit. "Say that you love me, Lindsay," I whispered. She slipped one soft bare arm around my neck, and laid her cheek to mine—" Dennis, dear, I love you! I love you! ifow could I help loving you when you were so good to me, when you saved my life, and when you for ever laid the Spectre of Durant Castle." THE END.

THE DOUBLE LIFE: OR, THE HAMPTON MYSTERY. By the Author of "DABIEWOOD. CHAPTER XLVII. THE FATHER AND THE LOVER. Such is the use and noble end of friendship, To bear a part in every storm of fate, And, by dividing, make the lighter weight. HIGGONH. If her enemy had been keen and swift in hig pursuit of Giralda, her lover had been no less keen and earnest. He had not known of her flight from Trevelyan Park until the next morning, when he had called, at the hour he had appointed, on his way to Lon- don. He had happened to arrive in one of Lord Tre- velyan's brief seasons of rest during his search, and the marquis, with haggard eyes, told him the story of the preceding evening's events; declaring that he had already thoroughly searched the neigh- bourhood, and that it was his opinion that Giralda had sought her mother in London, or her father at The Evergreens. "At any rate," said Lord Trevelyan, "I am sure that Pumpton is with her, and that she is safe. I will find her, if I have to hire a detective to search for her. lam going up to London." Lord Grosvenor made no attempt to combat this resolution, although, having more light on the subject of Giralda's family, he did not for an in. , stant believe that Giralda had gone to London. He had told her that The Evergreens was deserted, and she was not likely to go there. She would not, of course, imperil the Lady Beatrice by going to her. He decided in his own mind that Punipton had taken her to some secluded place where she would be safe from pursuit, and that he should hear from her at the earliest opportunity. "I should like you to let me know if you hear from her," said the marquis, in a troubled voice. "Of course all is over between you and he, Gros- venor. You will never link your name to that of the daughter of Geoffrey Trevelyan—" "And why not ? " interrupted Grosvenor, with flashing eyes. "I love her, and she loves me. As to her father, my lord, he is the noblest, the purest, the most injured of men. One of these days you will know the truth—that you have foully wronged one of the truest souls God ever made. It will be a terrible hour for you when the truth comes out." "The secret of Giralda's parentage, then, IS not new to you this morning ? " Lord Grosvenor replied in the negative. "Giralda told me all her story on the night I rescued her from the bluff cottage," he said. "She never knew that her father was Geoffrey Trevelyan . until you took her up to London. It was some strange providence that guided her to you, that made her love you. Her parents would sooner have suffered her to enter the den of a lion than enter your house." Lord Trevelyan's face paled, and his lip quiv- ered. "I think Giralda really loved me," he mur- mured. "Loved you, my lord! She never tired of sounding your praises. Her love for you was genuine, and she dreaded nothing more than being sent from you." The marquis averted his face. "You have seen him ? " he asked, after a brief space, in an altered voice—" Geoffrey ? " "I have seen him." There was another silence. Lord Trevelyan leaned more heavily on his staff, and continued to avert his face. "How does he look ?" he asked at length, huskily. "What does he say of me ?" "He looks older, and changed, of course, from the boy you knew, my lord. He looks like a true- hearted, pure-souled man. He said nothing of you." The marquis sighed heavily. "Giralda is not his only child!" he questioned, still in that husky voice. "She is not. Mr. Trevelyan has two sons. The younger is a fair-faced, golden-haired little fellow, who will make a poet some day," said Lord Grosvenor, pitying the lonely and misguided old marquis from his soul, and being willing to im- part the knowledge which he knew the vindictive old nobleman longed and thirsted to hear. "He ii a boy to love and cherish." "And the elder ? " "He is one to be proud of, my lord., He must be nearly fifteen, tall for his years, brave, manly, and spirited. He has your black eyes, your hair, your complexion. He is a true Trevelyan." "He looks like me!" and the marquis's voice fluttered with surprise. "Strange! What do they call him ? " "Rupert—Walter Rupert" "My name !" muttered the old lord, under his breath. "They have given their boy my name!" "And that fact alone proves Geoffrey Trevelyan's innocence !" cried Giralda's lover. "If he had been guilty he would not have named his son for you, when every utterance of the name must have ' brought back the memory of his attempted crime. Lord Trevelyan, you have been wronged and deceived most greviously, but the man who has wronged and deceived you is Lord Adlowe, and not Geoffrey Trevelyan." The marquis shook his head obstinately, reso- lutely conquering the softening emotions Lord Grosvenor had aroused. "Geoffrey has won an able advocate in you, my lord," he said, his voice gathering strength and harshness. "He has successfully imposed upon you. He cannot impose on me. I am made of sterner stuff. But if Giralda will return to me I will forbear to prosecnte him. I will promise no- thing, further. To forgive him would be impos- sible. ' Lord Grosvenor did not prolong the conversa- tion. He decided to defer his visit to town, and to engage in the search for Giralda, less because he feared she had not found safe refuge, than be- cause he feared Adlowe might trace her and molest her. From one of the park servants he found out the addresses of Plimpton's relatives, and those of the friends she was most in the habit of visiting, and during the day he called upon them, but of course without success. In the evening he returned to the Eyrie, half ex- pecting .to find a letter there from Giralda, but in this he was disappointed. At daybreak he was in the saddle again. This was the morning on which Lord Adlowe encountered Mrs. Plimpton, as she was coming out of the little town of Dalton, and it was on the even- ing of this day that Adlowe effected his recapture of Giralda and Fay. The day was passed by Lord Grosvenor in fruit. less search, and at evening he returned home only to be disappointed anew in his hope of a letter or message from his betrothed. On the third morning—that on which Giralda and Fay were brought to the lonely house near Pencoed, and on which also the Lady Beatrice re- ceived her daughter's letter and the exciting visits, the events connected with which we have recorded —Lord Grosvenor rode over to Trevelyan Park, which on the previous day he had avoided. He was anxious to learn if the marquis or Lord Ad- lowe had obtained news of Giralda. He learned from the butler that Lord Trevelyan had gone to town, and that Lord Adlowe had not been at the Park since the night of Giralda's dis- appearance, although his man Wigby had been there once or twice. •

Mrs. Pumpton had not returned. Tired with her long journey, and being overtaken by the mist, the worthy woman had halted over night at the house of a friend, and she arrived home about an hour after Lord Grosvenor had departed. This third day was a repetition of the two pre- ceding, and about sunset the young lord returned to the Eyrie, discouraged and disheartened, strange fears and forebodings struggling in his breast. " Adlowe must have captured her," he thought, despondently. "If she were free, she must have Written to me." He sat down in the pretty oriel parlour to which he had conducted Giralda on the night of her rescue from the bluff cottage, and stirred the fire. The rays of the declining sun came straggling in at the west window, warm and red, bringing the promise of a fine morrow. "'Where is she, my poor darling ?" he asked him- self, his noble young face glooming. "Oh I for some tidings of her I" • As if hi. Drayer had been heard, Mrs. Latten. the tousexeeper, entered at true juncture, nringmg a /otter. "It came this morning, directly after you went gut, my lord," she said. The young lord seized the letter, his heart tell- ing him who Was the writer. He waited a moment until the housekeeper had withdrawn, his eyes meanwhile devouring the delicate characters com- posing the address, and then he tore open the mis- sive. It proved, of course, to be from Giralda. It gave him her address, and assured him of her safety. After all his anxieties this letter had brought bim a great joy. Ee kissed it with all a lover's

rapture, his bright tears leil on it, and over it he murmured a host of tender words which, if Giralda had heard them, would have set her youdg nears throbbing. "1 shall set out by the first train in the morn- ing," he said. "I shall be in 1.).it0.11 with Giralda by noon.' He was longing impatiently for the morrow, when Mrs. Latten again entered, this time an- nouncing the arrival of visitors. "Visitors !" exclaimed her young master, in sur- prise ; and his thoughts immediately reverted to Giralda. "It is not a young lady, Mrs. Latttn— the young lady I brought here from the bluff cottage ?"

"No, my lord. It is an old gentleman, with a youug lad." Lord Grosvenor's face expressed disappointment. "Show them in I" he said.

He put away his letttr, and in a moment later the visitors were ushered in, Mrs. Latteu imme- diately withdrawing. The younger of the two visitors the young lord recognised at once as Gira.ida's brother Rupert. He hastened to meet him with outstretched hand, aud with words and looks of welcome.

Rupert's companion was a gentleman of appar- ently advance(' years. He wore spectacles and a His figure was tall and stout. and he leaned heavily upon a staff. He looked many years older than the " Professor " who had called on the Lady Beatrice Hampton, and the character of his looks was entirely different. Lord Grosvenor having greeted Rupert, turned his glances upon the lad's companion. "You don't recognise me, my lord ? " inquired the gentleman feebly, in a cracked voice. '1 do not sir," replied Grosvenor, politely, "but you are none the less welcome at the Eyrie. Any friend of—of Rupert, is my friend." The gentleman removed his spectacles, straight- ; ened his bent figure, and looked at Grosvenor with the wide, honest, truthful blue eyes he at once recognised. "Geoffrey Trevelyan ! " cried Paul in astonish- ment, ::rasping his visitor's hands. "This is a great pleasure, the more so for being so unexpected. What a clever disguise 1 I did not dream of ita being you!" "You thought us safely hidden at your secluded country-place, my lord," said Trevelyan, in his natural tones. "I believed that we were tracked there, and Rupert and I stole off to London where I effected my present disguise. We came here by a circuitous route, and I believe we have quite thrown off any pursuit." The young lord wheeled an easy chair to the fire and pressed Mr. Trevelyan to occupy it. "We have discovered Fay's whereabouts," said Trevelyan, as he leaned back on the cushions with a delicious sense of restfulness. "He is with Giralda." "Yes, I know," declared Grosvenor. "I have just received a letter from Giralda !" _ _

He exhibited it to his guest, who read and re- turned it.

"How did you learn of Fay's safety?" asked Grosvenor, restoring the letter to his breast- pocket. "I was at Hampton House yesterday," was the reply. "The Lady Beatrice had just received a letter from Giralda." The young lord cast a quick glance at Rupert, 'Who had seated himself on a couch, and was star- ing thoughtfully into the fire. The lad had changed of late. His face had grown grave and thoughtful, but it wore a cheerful expres- sion. His dark eyes, Grosvenor noticed, were full of a loving tenderness whenever he looked at his father, and his manner toward Trevelyan was ten- derly reverential and respectful. • One thing was certain. Geoffrey Trevelyan had found in his young son a friend who would stand by him, who would believe in him, who would love him, whatever might befall. Trevelyan intercepted Grosvenor's glance, and a fond, proud smile curved his melancholy lips. "Rupert knows all, Paul," he said, looking lov- ingly at his son. "I have told him the whole story. He is very anxious to become my defender, but I can rely on his prudence and discretion. You may speak freely before him." "Did you say you were at Hampton House ? " asked Grosvenor, in surprise. "Yes, I called there, disguised as a music- master. I had a long interview with the Lady Beatrice, and, on leaving her, I encountered in the hall Lord Hampton, my uncle, and a detective, the same Adlowe had employed. I had eyes only for Lord Trevelyan. How he has changed in eighteen 'ears!" And Trevelyan's face clouded. "He had no suspicion of your identity?" "I think not, Paul. Yet he stared at me as if fascinated. I fear I returned his gaze wtth in- terest. At sight of him a thousand memories thronged upon me. I remembered all his many noble qualities, and forgot his faults. It needed but a word of recognition, but a kindly look, and I could have fallen on his neck like the prodigal of old.

Trevelyan shaded his face with his hand. Evi- dently those old memories and that early affection were at that moment asserting themselves in his soul. _ _ _

Rupert seemed to struggle between his love for his father and his hatred for his father's enemy. He was very much like Lord Trevelyan in his character and temperament, but he had been so carefully trained that he could never commit the errors which had marred the life of his great- uncle.

"Father," he said, his voice full of feeling, "I could never forgive Lord Trevelyan, if I were you, until he had got down on his knees to me. I've got too much of the Old Adam' in me to forgive a man who could be so ready to believe evil of me. He never could have rightly loved you if he was ready to believe things against you." "You forget, my boy, said Trevelyan, "that appearances were strongly against me—that, in fact, his lordship found me clutching the hilt of the dagger which was buried in his shoulder. You can- not blame him, then, for believing the evidence of his own senses." "But Ido father," said Rupert, stoutly. "He ought to have heeded your denials. He ought to have known you incapable of such a crime. He ought to have suspected that Lord Adlowe of hav- ing contrived a plot." Trevelyan smiled faintly. "If all people loved like you, my impetuous Rupert," he said, "we should never hear of jealous suspicions, and mischief-makers would lose hall their occupation." "About that detective?" asked Grosvenor, anx- iously. "Are you sure he did not follow you, Mr. Trevelyan ? " "He followed me from Hampton House, as I expected, on seeing him, that he would. I took a cab, and he took one. I went into a shop, and passed out at its rear end while he was watching Its front. He could not watch on both streets, you know. Then I entered an omnibus, and went miles out of my way. I did not go to my lodgings until long after dark, and I am sure I had thrown the detective completely off the track. I did not like to leave town by any of the railway stations, lest I should meet my enemy; so, before day- break, I procured a cab, and Rupert and I pro- ceeded to an out of town station, and booked our- selves for Newport. At Newport, we booked our- selves for Cardiff, and from Cardiff we came over in a boat, which I bought of a fisherman, and which is moored at the foot of your rock stairs. I sailed the boat myself." "No one, after all these precautions, can track you here," exclaimed Grosvenor smiling. "You must be hungry." He rang the bell for supper. It was soon brought in and placed upon the round table, and the three gathered around the cheerful board. It was now about twilight. Mrs. Latten drew the shutters, let fall the curtains. linhted wax candles for the table ana the swinging lamp over- head, stirred the Ere to a brighter blaze, and with- drew, leaving host and guests to themselves. Trevelyan under the pleasant influences his host brought to bear upon him' grew more hopeful. He explained that the Lady Beatrice was about to go to Hamptonwold, and that he should call upon her there as soon as he could do so safely. He also confessed his perplexity in regard to the future of Giralda, declaring that he thought seriously of taking her and the boys abroad. "Our happy little home is broken up," he said. "It seems almost impossible to form another at present, while we are so watched.' The young Lord Grosvenor seized the opportu nity of urging a speedy marriage between himself and Giralda, and his arguments in its favour were so full and convincing that Trevelyan yielded his assent, and promised to place the matter in a favourable light before the Lady Beatrice. CHAPTER Ir.

I urn confess that it was a strange household, and one that required some acumen to understand. I had been an inhabitant of Durant Castle six weeks, and during those six weeks had learned news that made my position one of the greatest delicacy The information which Miss Lindsay Durant so solemnly gave on the day of my arrival, I treated at first witii ridicule, and brougut to bear upon the subject the glarish light of healthy criticism and unbelief. But since then my views had changed considerably, and I was inclined to think that some grave mystery was connected with Durant Castle.

Part of the news that I heard was to the effect that if Miss Lindsay died before reaching the age of twenty-one, the whole her immense fortune and property went to Evan Jones, who, it seemed, had been Mr. Durant's steward, and on his death was appointed Miss Lindsay's guardian. Another item of news told to me by Miss Durant herself was that Evan Jones had proposed marriage to her, and had been refused. The spectre had not been seen since my arrival, and under the influences of fresh company, Miss Lindsay soon regained some of her lost spirits. We had become great friends, the feeling on my side fast developing into a passionate absorbing love. Evan Jones was often .away from the castle on business connected with the estate, and sometimes did not return for a couple of days or so. He and I did not get on very well together; there was something shifty and wavering in his manner which did not favourably impress me, and his persistence in speaking of Miss Durant as .mad, aroused my suspicions. It was the evening of the 15th of February, 1883, Jones had been away from the castle since the pre- ceding morning on business, and was not expected home for a couple of dap, consequently, Lindsay and I were alone in the great drawing-room— except for Mrs. Bond, who, .good soul, went to sleep in a great arm chair before the fire directly after dinner. Miss Lindsay was at the piano, on which the candles were lit, their rose-coloured shades casting a soft glow over her lovely face. As • I watched her from my comfortable seat with half- closed eyes, I saw her glance towards me once or twice, and then evidently thinking that I, too, was asleep, she ceased playing, and, with her hands clasped loosely before her, came over to the fire. There was a look in the dusky, violet eyes when they rested on my face that caused my heart to beat fast with a delirious joy. Could it be that she was learning to care for sue? When we said " good- night" I ventured to press my Bps to the slender hand that lay so confidingly in 'nine, and the shy, startled look amply repaid me. I sat long over the glowing embers in my grate that evening, and must have dozed away, for I was aroused by a terrified scream from the direction of Miss Durant's room. Mrs. Bond, in dressing-gown and slippers, together with several other shivering domestics, was on the landing when I rushed out, I tried the door handle of Miss Durant'a room, only to find that it was fast. "Miss Lindsay, pray open the doer," I cried, and had the satisfaction of hearing the key turned in the lock. Flinging it open I sprang into the room, i in which a lamp was dimly burning. bliss Durant, in a crimson dressing-gown, from underneath which her pretty bare feet peeped white as snow-flakes, stood with her hand on the chimney-piece for sup- port, and such a dazed look of horror in her eyes that I involuntarily sprang to her side, said put my around her waist to prevent her falling. Mrs. Bond and the servants crowded in and attended to Miss Lindsay's maid, who had been sleeping with her mistress, and had fainted. "Miss Lindsay,' I said softly, "what startled you dear?" She lifted her eyes to my face full of that over- powering horror, and gradually they filled with tears that made them look like violets tipped with dew. "Oh I Denis," she whispered, my name falling from her lips so sweetly that I tightened my hold of the slender waist, "I have seen it again. It wrote five more days,'" she added, with a shudder. "Five more days, did it?" I answered grimly. "Then I, as your medical attendant, together with Mrs. Bond, will stay in this room until after the 21st of February, and if his ghostship appears whilst lam here thar'll be a file,' as Artcmus Ward said." -

A faint smile crossed the little April face so neur my own, and she nestled closer to my side, her silky hair brushing my face, as Mrs. Bond came fussing over to us. I impressed upon both her and Miss Lindsay the absolute necessity for keeping secret the fact that I intended to stay in the room at) night-time, and leaving the wornemolk together tur the rest of the night, I went back to my own rooms, and making up an immense tire thought out the whole affair.

A CHEMIST, expatiating on the discoveries in chemical science, observed that snow had been found to posse.s a considerable amount of heat. An Irishman was present (he always is on such occasions), and observed that " truly chemistry was a valuable science," and inquired of the orator what number of snowballs would be sufficient to boil a tea. kettle.

A CHARITABLE man wrote to a newspaper anon* the duty of helping the poor who ask for aid, and wound up by saying, "If you can give no- thing else you can at least give the applicant a little kindly word." The printer made it a little kindling wood," and like Shakespeare, "he build- ed better than he knew."

HEN RIK IBSEN.

The Norwegian dramatist has probably elicited more hostile criticism than any artist since Wagner, with whom, however, he is in no other sense to be compared.. Whatever fault may be found with his plays of modern lite on ethical or esthetic grounds no one who is familiar with his Brand and Peer Gynt, not to speak of his Em- peror and Galilean or of his other historic dramas, can deny that he is a great poet, nor will any fair-minded critic contend thar he is not a great satirist. Equally must his intimate knowledge of stage craft be admitted. This was acquired as director, first of the theatre at Bergen (1852-57), and then of the National Theatre, Christiania. Dr. Ibsen, who was born at Skien in 1828, has had more than the ordinary share of poverty and neglect, even in the case of innovators. But for years past he has had a large following in all parts of Europe, and even those who dislike his tone and methods to> much to give him a hearing, have perforce to read him. To one ot his ironic turn of mind the irreconcilable meanings that have been read into his productions must be highly amusing. What if the Tendenz theories of his pieces are all alike wrong?

===== 0002601_18941229_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
CHASED BY INDIANS.

• The Indians, in their ideas about their property in hunting-grounds, particularly detest through routes of the white men's communication. The telegraph and railway follow the wagon roads and express messengers' traeks,and all conduce to preventing the great periodi- cal migrations of game north and south. Hence the Pony Express Service was always menaced by them, no mat- ter what their tribe,and often were its riders attacked. "lhey had little chance of escape, except by running the gauntlet, es the Indians rarely fall on a fleet rider except when they can surround him with an over- whelming force. A party of them had stormed a mail-station and murdered the postmaster. They went into ambuscade at a little distance by Horse Creek. Thanks to a miracle, Bill Cody rode through them al- most before he knew ke was waylaid, and he little feared their chase until he observed that two of them were gaining on him, and dropping the others. A Pony Express rider is dressed as compactly as possible, and Iris nothing to aivest himself of to lessen weight, so that all the young man could do was to use whip and spur on his horse. But the two " reds " had large horses, such as are called " Americans," in con- tra-distinction to the under-sized native mustangs or ponies. One of the twain soon forged ahead of the other, showing that his horse, a roan, was much superior to the second, for he was not forcing it. Meanwhile Cody's bad become shaky, and, as it would never do to fall with it, "the trouble" had to be settled at once. Be stopped abruptly, wheeled, and fired with his ready revolver on this red man, who seemed by his feathers to be s chief. He was killed outright, before he could stir a finger. The horse threw him off, and Billy caught at the rein. To mount an fly were an easy matter; but the mails on his saddle were dearer than life. He alighted, and was hastily making the transfer, when the second savage dashed up and knocked off the rider's cap with a bullet. The pony-rider's two horses kept his hands full,but his own failing steed served him as a target to a second ball, which removed him out of his way. With two revolver-shots the second India* received his quietus. But now the other red-skins appeared in view. Cody cut the girths and took away his saddle upon the roan, and raced thence before the shots could reach him. At the station he did not find the shelter he expected. for the postmaster had been killed as already stated ; and so he had to keep on in his flight. Be arrived at no point of safety until Plontz Station, where be came in ahead of time—a troop of scalp-taking savages at one's rear forming the sharpest of goads l—" The World of Adventure."

"CAPITA GIIAT.

On Saturday afternoon, one Of the great father .of the co-operative mo‘ ement, Mr. E. T. Craig, was laid to his rest, in Hamm-I.:A:lit-1i Cemetery, in the presence of a numb,,r of own w:.11 known to the co-operative world, lie spent his life in pro- moting and pushing the co-operative principle. but he lived to see it in many ca,-es degrir led into Common huckstering and ordinary shop-keeping while parading before the wor1:1 the false plumes -of co-operative indisstry. ills life from his cradle to his grave almost, covers the whole century ; his active career on behalt of co-operation covers at, least 60 Or 70 years. lie was of Nianche,4er origin, and laboured for a time in Lancashire a: a writer and worker on behalf of the cause to which he was •devoting his energies and he must have witnessed With sorrow in his latter years how capitalist firms, With no love of the object he pursued, exploited ,Co-operation and produced so many travesries of the principle that only a trained student of co- •operative economies could disentangle the false from the true profit-sharing.

The news of Lord R. Churchill's collnipse has come more quickly on the back of my announcement of a few day ago than I anticipated. There is no doubt that a brilliant public career has been brought to an end ; a career which, with health and discretion, would have ended in the course of time in the attainment of the Premiership. When be and Mr. Balfour and their two or three colleagues at below the gangway as the Fourth Party, which • excited much jeering amusemrnt among politicians on both sides of the house, Lord Randolph held the lead, not only as the originator of the party, but as the most daring, inventive, and eloquent of the little group. Mr. Balfour did not then suggest that be would develop the fine parliamentary powers .which he has since shown himself to possess, and he has never, even to the present moment, so touched the sympathies and admiration of thjo de- democracy as Lord Randolph did in the height of his vigour. Lord Randolph's decline began with his quarrel with his colleagues in the Cabinet, his -resignation,and his long period of sulking in silence behind the Treasury Bench. While his old col- leagues were struggling with the Opposition he dropped out of public sight; and when he threw himself once more into the battle the disease which has now reached an acute stage had ruined his constitution, and Democratic Toryism was left with a few paltry •akirmishers, but without a chief.

There is something pathetic in the fact that dur- ing the next session he will be paired with Mr. Gladstone, his old protagonist. The two have fought many a doughty battle in the parliamen- tary arena and on the platform, but they were both warriors of chivalrous mould, and they fought with the courtesy of high-souled knights. In all the wind and fury ot Mr. Gladstone's eloquent passion, no unparliamentaiy discourtesy of expression ever -crossed his lips. Lord Randolph aimed at the same bigh strain of Parliamentary propriety. When he was in the full pos,ession of his strength,his mind was terser than Mr. Gladstone's*, his tongue more biting, and he was gifted with an incisive faculty of invective which was not part of Mr. Gladstone's mental equipment, for Mr. Gladstone's invective, when he wielded it, was rhetorical and not incisive, But a singular respect for each other was always shown by these two great debaters. It was the respect which one brave and skilful combatant bears for another. I am, of course, speaking -of some years ago, for latterly Lord Randolph's blows have been both aimless and feeble.

Lord Russell, who was a member of the Reporters' Gallery before he had a seat in the House of Com- mons, is not unmindful of the reasonableness of making the conditions under which newspoper men do their work as pleasant as possible. For years past the reporters in the Lord. Chief Justice's Court have laboured under the greatest difficulties, owing to the fact that the witness-box was removed from its original position near their allotted seats to the other side of the Court. In response to representa tions on the subject, the Lord Chief Justice has informed a deputation that the witness-box shell be restored to its old position, and he has directed 80020 Other necessary alterations to be made which will greatly conduce to the comfort of those who have to record the proceedings in Court. Now that the head of the division has set the example, there is little doubt that the lot of the reporters in other Queen's Courts will be made more agreeable than hitherto.

in the Tate spring or early summer of next year &small but well-equipped expedition will. I learn, leave England for the purpose of exploring the interior of Nova Zembla. the object being to collect the fullest possible information relative to the geography, geology, botany, and natural history of the islands. I believe that there will only be four members of the expedition, but each man will he thoroughly competent to undertake some special branch of investigation. Two of the m?mbers of the party are medical men, one of whom is organis- ing the expedition, and will have control of the general management and look specially after the zoology department. The other medical man has acted as surgeon on board whaling ves.els. and has consequently considerable experience in Arctic life. To him the botany of the country will be entrusted. The other two members of the expedition are a retired officer of the Royal Navy, and an artist who has travelled widely. The former is a thoroughly Competent nautical astronomer and geologist. It will thus go hard with the party if they do not succeed in collecting sufficient material to add greatly to our knowledge of this Antic land, the shores of which are fairly well known, though the interior is for the most part a erra incognita.

Although Madame Sarah Grand has not, been a prolific writer of books,it is probable that the books Bhe has written have taken much nerve power out of her and been a severe tax upon her energies. It is with regret, therefore, I hear that her health has given way, and that she is compelled to alvindon for a time her literary work. She will seek recupera- tion in the more genial climate of the south of Spain,where she will escape the cutting east winds of the English spring. She will take with her the best wishes of all readers of her works, whether they are sympathisers with the new movement, or not. Beginning her literary life with the greatest modesty, she has been forced into prominence by her own genins,and it is to be hoped that a woman of her originality and power will yet produce many works which will enrich English literature.

The energy with which the Japanese are striving to extend their foreign trade is not less than that which they throw into their military operations. They now have consuls everywhere. Their grent. t3overnment steamship line has gradually extendad frum the Japanese ports to those of Corea and China, to Hong Kong and Singapore, and quite re- cently to Bombay. The main object of this Bombny extension is to convey the cheap Indian raw'cotton to the Japanese mills, and to take a share also in the carriage of lndinn cotton goods to Chinn. In pnrsuance of this plan for extending the trade of Japan with Jndia, a Japanese consul has been ap- pointed to Bombay, and a branch of the Yokohama Specie Bank has been opened there. The new Con- sul is Mr. Daid,lro Goh, who has been for seven years in the Japanese consulate in London, and who is one of the shining lights of the Japan Society. He thinks that the great aim of Japanese spinning mills at present is to supply the home demand, and that, in spite of the progress of the industry in Japan in recent years, it will be long before they will be able to do it. Abundance of raw cotton grown in Japan is wholly insufficient for the half million spindles now at work. Hence the new steamship line, which will also pid in carry- ing Japanese coal throughout the east.

LADIES' LETTER

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

There has not been muoh need up to the present for furs and thick outdoor clothing, but you will not be far wrong in purchasing at once. 1 suppose the general "awfulness" of the summer has made people ready for all sorts of weather. Certain it is that lots of furs are being sold. Small fur oollarettes will probably be worn throughout the winter, and you cannot do better than get one in either sable or skunk. I should advise you to avoid one with either head or paws; a tail each end is the nicest finish. The newest collarettes are longer than those waioh have been worn dur- ing the early winter, being indeed, short, fiat boas. Little tepee are now made entirely of fur. A pretty one is made of light sable, and has a huge erect bow of old-pink velvet at the left side, and two lappets of cream lace falling on the hair at the back. THIII-0' Slantere in fox, and in caracole and astrachan, are also seen ; in each ease only decorated with a bookie and quill at o e side. In regard to clanks, sable, if you can afford it, or sealskin are qu'he the right materials. If you intend buying the former material, now is the right time to do so, for I hear that there is a great depreciation in the value cf sealskin. Chinchilla is to the fore again, and is seen on jackets and capes of darker for. Oa sealskin and caracole, a chinchilla collar and revere form a trimming, but this, in my opinion, is not nearly so becoming as a darker fur. S:noke fox is a popular favourite, and is al excellent match to the smoke-grey frieza coats which are one of this ti( aeon's latest productiocs. A I said a moment ago, sedibkin is the best thing for mantles. A good model of this kind is made of Alaska seal. skin. It droops nearly to the knees anu is a simple shape, with high turned-down collar. A square Polish cap of the same material, with a brush osprey, completes a very smart get-up.

No doubt you would like to know what Is being worn in the way of ball-dresses. The most popular material is, unquestionably, satin, and the embroideries which decorate this are positively wondrous. They are usually executed on a piece of satin or velvet, out into oonventional designs, and outlined with pearls or gold or coloured spangles. Pendant fringes are also very much in vogue, and it is quite usual to find points of em- broidery decorating either hip of a plain skirt. Chiffon is improved by infinitesimal sequins run all over it, and this makes a &arming bodice in the overhanging style, with braces of chiffon with kilted ends. The newest chiffon sleeves are formed of innumerable tucks run round by hand; but there is a tendency to curtail the sleeves. Russet-coloured cloth makes a very nioe dress for a girl of about 16. A girl of that age who was wearing a dress of that colour struck me as looking exceedingly smart. The costume was composed of a blouse and skirt. The former was full with wide sleeves, and was made of spot Surah, the tone being a poppy-russet. The skirt was of russet cloth, reaching to the ankles. It also forms a pointed bodice, edged with passementerie of guipure, leather-gilt, and poppy colour.

Hats are trimmed for the most part—the smaller ones, that is—with bows which cross the hat literally from side to side, and the trimmings are arranged in a formal style, which is so greatly in the interest of the home milliner, for some milli- nery house!) prepare these trimmings all ready to put on the hat, and the whole process resolves Itself into two or three firm stitches at the back and front. The large spreading hat of.the picture type, which is so extremely becoming on a tall figure, or to a piquant or youthful face, requires a more careless and graceful arrangement, but is by no means too difficult to be made at home. The wide flat type, with a low round crown, in felt, can be bought for a couple of shillings in the new colours. Common felts are, however, stiff and hcavy, and it is poor economy to purchase the cheaper makes, as the soft ones can be re•blooked, to a newer style, when they become old- fashioned, and they also retain their colour

to th the commoner ones fade quickly. But toques are what I should advise you to get. One in blue velvet has a border of black ostrich tips all the way round, and very handsome jet ornaments here and there, this being made in that wide shape so extremely popular just now. Yours lovingly, JENNIN.

As an example of women's colrage a story of a brave school teacher comes trorn India. Miss Lucind Gore, of Lackharabad, was standing in hi r schoolhouse one day, when, turning round, she saw in the open doorway the glaring eyes of a tiger. She did not scream or faint, but seized a broom and drove it full in the tiger's face, brush foremost. Some of the bristles entered the tiger's eyes, and with a howl of pain he drew back to make a spring at her. Then she slammed the door in his face. The supports of the door were but stender, and would not resist the tiger's attacks long, but, while lie banged his head against it, she lowered one of her boys through the window to alarm the neighbours. Assistance soon arrived and the tiger was shot.

Why'Kitchen Boilers Explode. As the winter season is now fast approaching, this opportunity is taken of bringing forward the subject of ciiculating boiler explosions, a great number of which occur during the usual annual run of frosty weather. . . . These explosions included, amongst other types, saddle, annular, vertical,rectangular, and tubular boilers. The latter type, however, appears to favour explosion of a much greater extent than the others, no less than fourteen of the total number being constructed of tribes. 119ferring to the cause of thee disasters, they were, in nearly every case, brought about through lighting a fire in the boiler when the pipe: were choked with ice. This illustrates the importance of keeping a good fire burning in the apparatus during frosty weather. By so doing a continuous circulation of hot water is kept running through the whole range of pipes and fri:ezing of the water preventei to a great extent. In most of the above cases the heating apparatus had not ben working for sever il days before the frost appeared. On its arrival the circulating pipes became choked with ice, so that when a fire was lighted there was no outlet for the gradually accumulating pressure, the result being a terrift explosion.—" Work."

Sleep. A man who works hard must have plenty of rest, and, as a'rule, his sleep should be unbroken and undisturbed. The a •tuA number of hours' sleep taken and necessary hr persons in health varies within wide limits according to age, the soundness of the sleep itself, and individual rcculiarity. Most of us work during the day and sleep .at night ; but some people, from the nature of their occupations, are compelled to work at night and sleep during the day. Even to the rule that slemp should be ccntinuous and uninterrupted there are exceptions. Worry means broken rest at night, or unrefreshing sleep, and then the strain must be relaxed, or the health will give way. Infants often sleep twenty hours out of the twenty-four, and children, as a rule, require very much more sleep than adults. In persons from fifteen to twenty-five, eight hours' sleep are necesvary, but people in the prime of life de very well with seven hours rest, or even less. Some people of energetic tempera- ment get along very well with five hours' Sleep out of the twenty-four ; but this is exceptional. Under certain conditions, the amount of sleep taken may be excessive. In idiots and persons who are more or less demented, the greater part of the day may be passed in sleep. These people, when their simple wants are satisfied, dream away their time, and are oblivious of all that goes on around them. After a prolonged mental effort many notable people have been known to pass from twenty- four to thirty-six hours in bed ; and shop girls and others who are hard-worked during the week not infre- quently sleep the greater part of Sunday, not leaving their room until late in the evening, when they take a stroll, have supper, and then again retire to rest. There is a curious disease which occurs as an epidemic, and to which the term "sleeping sickness" has been applied. The somnolence may last from six to twelve months,and is often so intense that it is with difficulty the patient is aroused to take nourishment. The exact nature of the complaint has not been worked out, but it commonly terminates fatally.—" The Family Physician."

SUCCESS OF A RUNCORN ENGINEER IN QUEENSLA D.

(C0:11:11UNICATED).

A public journalist always feels an interest in the well-doing of his old readers, and especially of such of them as may happen to have emigrated to -far off lands and there made for themselves distinguished and honoured reputations ; and the " Examiner," I am sure. is not an exception in this respect. I am convinced that it will be a pleasure to ,you to puruse and publish an illustration in point ; and as an old Cheshire journal- ist (attached to the " Chronicle " over a quarter of a century ago, under the editorship of Mr. Fred W. Mar- shall, (now the eminent Q.C., I am proud to see), I am glad to be the hand to supply it. The subject is Mr. A. Wilson Littler, head of the firm of Littler and Bruce, Fitzroy Foundry, Rockhampton, whose father was formerly and for 14 years manager of the dockyards belonging to the Bridgewater Navigation Company, Limited, at Runcorn, and is, I believe, occupied in a similar position in Runcorn at the present moment, under the Great Manchester Ship Canal Company. Mr. Wilson Littler served his apprenticeship in the Bridge- water Company's Works under his father, and after working a few years as a journeyman he resolved to come out to Australia. Arriving in Roe,khatnpton in 1883, soon after the British India Steam Navigation Company had commenced running their mail steamers through to Queensland, and made the harbour of Rock- ampton one of their principal ports of call, Mr. Littler saw at once that, although there existed already one large foundry in the chief city on the Fitzroy, there was room for another, although possibly, to establish it might prove a tough undertaking. But Mr. Littler had come out quite prepared to meet diffieulties—and, what was better, to fight and conquer them. Shortly after his arrival in 1883—only just eleven years ago—and I well remember the time—he entered into partnership with Mr. Lund, and commenced operations on a moder- ate scale at what has since expanded into the extensive Fitzroy Foundry, which, next to the great Meat Pre- serving Works of Lake's Creek that supplies France. China, and other foreign powers with meats for their armies—forms the most prominent land mark in Rock- hampton as you approach it from the ocean, and is a splendid s!te for the development of what will, I have little doubt, eventually swell out into a large ship- building business. In a short time Messrs. Lund and Littler (as the firm was originally known), finding their business increasing, took into partnership Mr. John Bruce, who had been for years in the employ of the Queensland Government as a marine engineer, and had always held the reputation of a first-class officer in the Marine Department. Later on Mr. Lund went out of the business, commencing "on his own hook" as a niechanical engineer in another part of the town.

About this time that wonderful golden rock, now justly celebrated among the wonders of the great mineral discoveries that have distinguished the Victorian Era—the Mount Morgan Gold Mine—disclosed its auri- ferous wealth, and began to pour out its marvellous stream of golden treasure. A great demand sprang up for mining machinery, and with considerable enterprise and foresight Messrs. Littler and Bruce went to work to add to their plant so that they might share in some of the good things that were likely soon to offer front the mining revival that had set in. Nor were they without their reward. Having been entrusted with the construc- tion of the chlorination plant for Mount Morgan, and turned out their contract to the complete satisfaction of the Company and in tt way that secured all the results anticipated from the chlorinating process of treating the ore, their fame extended over all Australia; orders came in from some of the principal gold fields on the continent, and for a fairly lengthened period Messrs. Littler and Bruce were kept going for more than full time an what ' may be called good renumerative work. They made money; and, wisely—as will be seen from what I am about to relate—they laid it out in expanding the pro- ductive capabilities of their foundry, thus aaain mani- festing the keen foresight which has distinguished them from the moment that the present firm was consolidated. About four months ago the directors of the Mount Morgan Company determined to make a large addition to their plant in the shape of three revolving furnaces for roasting the ore. They called for tenders. fixing the , the time for completion of the work within 14 weeks from the signing of the contract. Only two tenders were sent in—one by the old firm of Burnes and Twigg, and the other by Littler and Bruce. The figure asked by the , latter was between £BOO and £7OO higher than that submitted by the older firm, hut the time stipulated by them for the delivery of the furnares came within that fixed by the Company, whereas Burnes and Twiag wanted 28 weeks; and as the furnaces were urgently , needed, time WAS made the essence of the contract, and I theY higher priced tender of Littler and Bruce was accepted. To give you an idea of the vastness of the work I may mention that in the construction of the -furnaces fully 200 tons of iron were used, and that the number of the castings, varying from a few hundred- weights to between four and flve,tons, was nearly 300. Each of the three furnaces had to he 42ft. in length with a diameter of Tft. Sin, tapered to tift. 81n. ;and that each furnace had to be in eight sections, and the massive wheels or tyres on which they were to revolve bolted between the sections. When the contract was com- menced between 50 and 60 hands ware put on and kept at work night and day, the nature of the work prevent- ing anything like regular shifts being observed; indeed it was such a huge undertaking that few people, except the contractors themselves, had any hope of its success- ful completion within the stipulated time. The" Morning Bulletin," the leading journal of Cen- tral Queensland and which is not too favourably dis- posed towards Messrs. Littler and Bruce, writing on the 23rd instant (October) had the following notice of the completion of the work: " A considerable amount of in- terest has been taken in the contract entered into by Messrs. Littler and Bruce, of the Fitzroy Foundry, Rockhampton, with the Mount Morgan Gold Mining Company for the building of three revolving furnaces for roasting ore. The chief condition of the contract, it will be remembered, was time; and although the firm were paid a price largely in excess of what would have been paid for like work under ordinary circumstances, some curiosity was felt as to whether they would be able to carry through the undertaking. The work was of considerable magnitude; the material required for it was scarce both here and in the southern colonies; and the plant the firm possessed was nst equal to the com- pletion of such a contract in the time stipulated. But, notwithstanding all these disadvantages, Messrs. Littler and Bruce have virtually fulfilled their contract. Yester- day they should have delivered the last part of the job at the company's works: and all but about thirty tons— out of a total of about 200 tons—is already there, and the balance will be on the ground on Thursday. This balance of thirty tons was all loaded on the railway trucks yesterday—the date when the contract time ex- pired—and nothing remains for full completion of th e aereemeut but the transport of this loading tn- Mount, Morgan. This is a feat of which Messrs. Littler and Bruce may be deservedly proud, especially when the difficulties they have encountered are considered. The inadequacy of their plant to complete such a large work in the time stipulated was the main difficulty, and it was not until nine of the fourteen weeks allowed them for the whole contract had expired that they were able to cast the first of the twenty-four heavy cylinders. Then they had to stop casting fbr about eight days—five days at one time, three days at another—through want of iron. Local supplies were totally exhausted, what little was procurable in the south had been Ns-nicht up, vessels that weie expected were late in arriving, and when, at length, this diffietiltis, appeared to be about at an end, the bargne Cambuswallace—with a large ship- ment on board—was wrecked almost within sight of Brisbane. The time lost in adding to the plant was, of course,allowed for in estimating the probabilities of their carrying out the work ; hut the delays caused in pro- curing iron could not be calculated, and but for this, we are assured, the contract would have been finished within the time. Townspenple generally will join in con- gratulating Messrs. Littler and Bruce on the successful completion of their big undertaking ; and, at, the same time, express the hope that they will reap the reward, their enterprise and determination entitle them tn." _ . _

Their treatment consisted of a little silent pressure being trought to bear upon them by an unpretentious looking machine that might well he christened the pig persuader. From the‘centre of the face of this.machine a bllt of iron about eight inches square, kept protruding, with what seemed a soft,silent, lazy motion that. however,represented the concentrated essence of a business-like squeeze.carrying, as it did' a4O ton pressure. The pig was placed in front of it, the bolt came out and touched it, and the length of that pig was lessened by a font as expeditiously and almost as silently as the teeth of a child decapitate a eingerbread horse. The iron in its reduced lengths is then hoisted to the furnace, into which it is placed, one layer of iron to one of coke. ! Two circular pits had been due' into the floor of the moulding shop, 7ft deep and 12ft in diameter. Fitting into these were the moulds for the castings, the prepara- ' tion of whicn was in itself a task requiring skill and caution. Built of bricks, iron and mould in sections strongly bolted together, they make a total weight when completed of between 12 and 13 tons each. Inside these are lowered the cores, which are b.ilt on iron cylinders being put together in segments and also strongly bolted. On the face of them are numerous small knobs for holding a cover of loam, the surface of which is made perfectly smooth. Before be- ing lowered into the pits both cores and moulds are wheeled into immense ovens kept at almost a white heat, the object being to remove all the moisture,a little Precaution necessary to avoid such a trifling mishap as the whole of the works being blown across the river by an explosion of the steam that would otherwise be generated by the molton metal. All this having been done, and moulds and cores in position in the pits, a space is between them to receive the liquid iron and form the castings. Everything being ready the con- tents of the furnace are liberated and flow down spouts, rivulets of fire, into the huge ladles. The scene at this stage is a grand one. The brief twilight has given place to the darkness of night, illumined only by the glare from the furnaces, and the red heat of the fast- flowing metal, which, as it runs over the edge of the spout into the glis'ening mass in the monster cabldron, throws up immense sparks, equalling in brilliancy a pyrotechnic display. Then the crane moves the ladle into position over the pit, a few turns of a small wheel cants over toe ca.uldron,and with a fierce hissing sound, broken by short sharp reports, and accompanied by my• riads of sparks moving in the air like immense fireflies, the stream of lurid metal rushes into the receptacle pre pared for it. A dozen stalwart forms with bared arms, and faces that seem set and stern in the glow, move hither and thither round the pit, out of which spring hundreds of jets of gas; a layer of sand is thrown over the seething mass, the red heat is hidden, and then one of the presiding sons of Vulcan taps the surplus metal out of the running box ; the cold iron he uses for the purpose meets the molten metal which, coming into con- tact with the gas and moisture in the sand, causes a miniature explosion, and there belches forth to the roof of the room a stream of sparks that, cooling in their descent,come down like a hail of shot—the work is done, the casting left to ccol. The pressure within is immense, the metal putting a strain on each mould equal to 24,0001 b. requiring very strong bolting to resist it. When the castings are cool enough to allow the cores to be lifted—and they are red hot when they are taken out— the immense weight causes the powerful chains to snap like thread, with a report somewhat trying to the nerves of the uninitiated. A few hours later the castings them- selves are exhumed, and what a little while before was a stream of running fire is represented by two immense. cylinders, ready to be carried to the machine room to be fixed on the lathe and both their ends machined true: pieces of a huge machinery, the workmanship of every part of which is a credit to the Fitzroy Foundry and the city of which it is so important an industry." Although Mr. Littler still retains the active manage- ment of the affairs of the firm it affords me very great pleasure to add that he takes a great interest in the pub- lic affairs of his adopted home. About three years ago he had the honour to receive an influential requisition from the ratepayers of his ward to stand as a candidate for its representation in the city Council, and although he had for his opponent a much older resident and an ex-Mayor, he was returned with a very respectable majority, and has proved himself one of the most intern- . gent and valuable members of the aldermanic body. Be is also a member of the Council of the Central Queens- land Territorial Separation League, the object Of which is to obtain home rule for the central province of the colony, a boon which I think likely to be granted to us in the near future, oer claim to autonomy having been unqualifiedly admitted by both Lord Ripon and Lord Knutsford, past and present secretaries for the colonies. This week a new honour was bestowed upon Mr. Littler by the Government of Queensland by the addition of his name to the Commission of the Peace for the territory, and I may mention that there is every likelihood of his being raised to the Mayoral dignity at an early date. Mr. Littler was married about eight years ago to Miss S. Evans, only daughter of Mr. Richard Evans, a retired professor of music, and tbis lady is now the happy mother of a room full of as bright a family of little boys as any proud father could wish to be carressed and sur- rounded by.

When I had everything in readiness, he would come with me to inspect the result of my labours before dressing for dinner, and no word except of praise ever came to my ears. When the house was full of visitors, he had a peculiar fancy for having the menu of the day's dinner placed on the sideboard at luncheon time ; and then he would discuss it with the guests in his quaint, humorous way, something like this: " Cock-a-leekie ? Good, decidedly good. Fried soles with shrimp sauce ? Good again? Croquettes of chicken ? Weak, very weak; decided want of imagination here.' And so on; and one woutd imagine that he lived for nothing but the coming dinner. On the contrary, he had a small but healthy appetite,and was remarkably abstemious in both eating and drinking."
