The Princess of Wales and her Millinery..

Perhaps few of her sex have ever better realised the value of a due knowledge of the lines of beauty in the female figure, and her success in rendering her dress in- variably becoming is due in a great measure to this. With so small a head, so beautifully shaped and so well placed on the shoulders, it would have been suicidal to have adopted any large or wide head-covering ; and what we all recognise as the Princess shape of bonnet is one which covers in the most fascinating manner the space between the middle of the fringe of hair worn now in front, and the knot,chignon, or coil that appears at the back. Nothing is ever well done without thought and trouble, and the Princess Alexandra devotes both, as also a cultured experience, to the snhject of dress. Before she decides what to wear, she often has several dresses, with bonnets and accessories, placed on stands for her to see and exactly judge of their effect. Of her three daughters,the Duchess of Fife resembles her moth- er the most closely, both in her carriage and the size of her head. The mother and her two unmarried daughters lead a most simple, natural life in their country home ; and though on state occasions in London you see the young people generally in bonrets, in the country they affect the same close shape of hats as their mother, and it is only this season that they have ordered any cf the larg( r forms which are current in present fashions. There is a pretty portrait of the three sisters together, taken when they were in mourning for their uncle the Duke of Albany, all dressed alike in round hats made of gathered velvet, cut on the cross and trinsmed with feathers.—" Cassell's Family Magazine."

WHERE DO THE UNSOLD PICTURES GO?

"So you would like to know what becomes of the pictures whieh are left unsold at the Academy and other exhibitions ?" observed a dealer in a large way to a, writer in " Cassell's Saturday Journal." " Well, per- haps I can enlighten you. To begin with, the Academy pictures that do not sell while en exhibition are usually kept by the artists themselves. But at some of the other shows, where pictures are exhibited much more for sale than for the honour of the thing, a different destination often awaits the pictures which don't •go off.' At the time when pictures are to be taken away,touts approach the artists and offer them, on behalf of their employers, a moderate price for the pictures. Thus secured, they are stored in the warehouse of some dealer, who, as a general rule, disposes of them ta some American specu- lator, who makes a gross bid for two or three hundred pictures, iri2W3e. These are shipped off to the New World, where they are put up separately to auction, and sold to the highest bidders, the pictures eventually being distributed over all parts of the United States. The pictures, as a rule, go very cheap; and it is no uncommon thing for a painting once exhibited in some London gallery to eventually hang unnoticed on the wall of an obscure Californian liquor-store or digger's hut."

D:CKENS AND CHRISTMAS.

Miss Marie Dickens, eldest daughter of the great novelist, has lately been interviewed by a contribuL.or to the excellent Christmas number of "The Young Man " and "The Young Woman." In the course of the con- versation, she gave" a bright and enthusiastic account of Dickens' observance of the Christmas season," remark- ing : "To my father it was a time dearer than any other part of the year, I think: He loved Christmas for its deep significance as well as for its joys. This he shows in every allusion in his writings to the great festival, a day which he considered should be fragrant with the love that we should bear one another, and with the love and reverence of his Saviour. In our childish days my father used to take us, every 24-h day of December, to a toyshop in Holborn, where we were allowed to select our Christmas presents, and also any that we wished to give to our little companions. Although I believe we were often an heur or more in the shop before our several tastes were satisfied, he never showed the least impatience." Miss Dickens has, of course, much to say concerning the Christmas festivities at Travistock House. The fol- lowing interesting anecdote is not to be found in Forster's " Life " _ . _

_ "When the 'boys' came home for the holidays there were constant practices in dancing for the Christmas and New Year parties. Just before one of these dances my father insisted that my sister Katie and I should teach the polka to him and Mr. Leech. My father was as much in earnest about taking that wonderful step cor- rectly as though there was nothing of greater import- I ance in the world. Often he would practise gravely in a corner without either partner or music. I rrmember, one cold winter's night; his awakening with the fear that he had forgotten the step so strong upon him that, jumping out of bed, by the meagre illumination of the old-fashioned rushlight, dtad to his own whistling, he diligently rehearse d its "one, two, three," until he was more secure in his knowledge. You can't imagine our excitement and nervousness when the evening came on which we were to dance with our pupils. Katie was to have Mr. Leech, who was over six feet high, for her partner, while my father was to be mine. My heart beat en fast that I could scarcely breathe, I was so fear- ful for our success." "What." asked the interviewer, "are your recollec- tions of Christmas at Gad's Hill, Miss Dickens ?" "Oh, I think that was the happiest time of all. Our house was always filled with guests, while a cottage in the village was reserved for the bachelor members of our holiday party. My father always deserted work for the week, and that was almost our greatest treat. He was the fun an ilife of those gatherings,the true Christ- maa spirit of sweetness and hospitality filling his heart. Long walks with him were daily treats to be remem- bered. Games made our evenings pass all ton rapidly. ' Proverbs ' was very popular, and likewise 'Dumb Crambo,' in which my father's great imitative ability was strikingly shown. I remember one evening his dumb showing of the word ' frog' was so extremely laughable that the memory of it sometime later con- y Ned Marcus Stone, the artist, when he tried to give his recollections of it." . .

Dickens had done so much to apotheosise the Christ- mas dinner that great interest attaches to what Miss Dickens has to say concerning this event in her father's household. _ _ .

The pudding had its own special dish of col«ured repousse china, ornamented with holly. THE SOLDIEWS-LIST WORDS.

" Brethren, let us dwell together in harmony and peace. _

_ These are good words, always fit to be spoken and they are especially fragrant as coming from lips which Death is about to seal for ever.

We quote the following from an American newspaper: "Henry D. Les, a prominent politician, while address- ing a meeting at South Norwalk, on October 31st, dropped dead from heart disease. His last words were, • Brethren, let us d-vell together in harmony and peace.' He was a well-known Grand Army man." Had this old soldier fallen from a rifle-ball through the brain his end could not have been more sudden. The paper says he died of heart disease. Is it likely he did ? Not at all likely, inasmuch as real heart disease is a very rare malady. More people are killed by lightning than by that. True heart disease is a shrinking of the lining membrane of the heart, caused by previous inflarama tion ; the inflammation being produced by rheumatism and gout, and the latter by the poisons generated in the stomach by indigestion and dyspepsia. As we have said, this mAlady is very rare ; a person may have a and live to be a hundred years old. His heart had simply lost power to pump as much blood as it did once; that's all. He must take life easier.

But the ailment that goes by the name of " heart I disease," is quite another thing, Women can explain the difference even better than men. Read this for example :—"When I was 17 years old, I seemed to lose my health all at once. It was in the summer of 1889 I that I began to have spells of feeling faint and giddy. My tongue was furred, my appetite poor, and after eat- ing I had pain in the stomach, and was all the time belching up wind. I was always tired and weak, and ani none the less so for eating; food didn't strengthen me as it used to do. " One day, in the latter part of the following October, while in service at Mrs. Firth's, Park Farm, Thornhill, I made a visit home. When I got there I had such pain and fluttering at the heart, I could scarcely stand. Tnis frightened my mother, as she got some of the neighbours to help me to the doctor's. He said, Your heart is in an alarming condition; you will have to be careful. On no account must you hurry or tnaks any violent effort.' "The doctor's medicines did me no good; I got worse and gave up my situation. Soon afterwards I had a nasty cough, and an irritation at the throat and chest that wouldn't let me sleep. I would sit up in bed till nearly daybreak, coughing, and spitting, and was worse tired than when I went to bed. My legs trembled so with weakness I couldn't stand or walk much, and had to have help to wash or dress myself. Well, this is the way I got on, month after month. One day mother thought I was dying, and ran and fetched Mrs. Senior, a neighbour. "The next January (1890) Mr. Kilner, of Messrs. Kilner Bros., Glass Bottle Manufacturers, Thornhill, Lees, recommended me to the Dewsbury Infirmary 'where I stayed six weeks; but the doccer's physic did no reit good. I kept wasting away, and people said thPre was no chance for me to get well. "It was then I first heard of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup. I read of its curing a case like mine—read of it in a little book. My mother sent for the Syrup. She bought it of Mr. J. Day, the chemist, at Thornhill, Lees. The first few doses made me feel better. The pain at my heart was easier, and my food agreed with me. So I kept on taking the Syrup and getting better. Presently I was strong enough to go to work. My colour came back too, and I have been well and all right ever since. If we could have afforded it we should hate put the parti culars of my ease in the newspapers. (Signed) Hannah Milnes, 18, Mapes Buildings, Brewery Lane, Thornhill, Lees, October 12th, 1892."

Now what is the commonsense of Miss Milnes' experi- ence? What was her ailment ? It was indigestion and dyspepsia. The heart trouble was one of the symptoms of the stomach trouble. Virtually, this is the founda- tion fact about " hear: disease," "heart failure," and all other manic disturbances. Cure the cause with Seigel's Curative Syrup and the results will pa se away with it.

THE ORIGIN OF THE TOP HAT.

The evolution of costume during the Victoria era was, we read in " Cassell's Illustrated History of England,' in the direction of simplicity. Gentlemen's Court dress retained the cocked-hat and knee-breeches of the eigh- teenth century, but the lace cravats and diamond shoe- buckles disappeared, and for variously-coloured silk coats was substituted, about 1845, a dark brown cloth or black velvet. With regard to morning wear the traditions of the Regency were preserved, at the beginn- ing of the reign, in the dark blue or green frock-coat, the high and voluminous neckcloth, the gorgeous waist- coat and the Hessian boots. Another style of dress was a military coat, frogged with braid and velvet. Of an evening, coloured coats and trousers, the former with velvet collars and the tails fared with silk.though going out of fashion, were still worn. This magni- ficence was, however, a survival ; and before the " fifties "had waned,black cloth became the rule both by night and by day. An easential feature of modern costume appeared about 1840, when the tall silk hat in- vaded England from France, and promptly replaced the beaver. This unbecoming, and inconvenient head- gear, though its shape varied from time to time, con- tinued to exercise a relentless despotism. In most respects the business man of 1847 looked much like hi, snccessor of forty years later; though the more elderly clung to the stock instead of the silk tie, worn first in a bow and afterwards in a sailors's knot, and gloves were invariably black, not tan-coloured. The details of the orthodox suit underwent, of course, numerous modifica- tions ; the coat was worn now double and now single- breasted, waistcoats were cut now high and now low, trousers varied from loose to tight. The unofficial dress became altered in the directism .f comfort. The Prince Consort popularised the ugly but useful felt hat or " billycock " ; coloured cloth suits were to be seen in country tlwns even on market days,though the City and Bond-street knew them not ; and the round tailless coat was no longer confined to boyhood.

HOW PENDULUMS ARE COMPENSATED,

A rise in the temperature is accompanied by lengthening of the pendulum rod, and the clock accord- ingly loses, just in the same manner as it would if the bob had been lowered by means of the regulating screw. The lengthening of the pendulum rod through heat being the main cause of the clock losing, it is evident that if a material could be found, and used in its construction, that did not expand under the action of heat, there would be no temperature error worth considering. The nearest approach to these conditions is found in a pen- dulum cemposed of a piece of thoroughly well-seasoned wood, with a straight grain running lengthwise er a trifle aslant, and a heavy round bob of no great height. Such pendulums are nearly always found in church clocks of the common kind, also in some regulators. and - in "Vienna" clocks, and the results are very satisfac- tory, though not quite free from error. Clocks fitted with these pendulums lose a little on being subjected to 'heat, and gain in the cold. It has therefore been sug- gested to compensate the remaining error by fitting the plain wood rod with a lead bob in the shape of a thick tube about one-quarter or one-third of the length of the pendulum, and resting at its lower end on the regulating screw. THE " Middleman " is an alto-,, The "Middleman" gether necessary parnage in the present state of society, yet, use- ful and necessary as he is, he is much abused. Everybody seems to be agreed—with the ()seep- tion, of course, of the middleman himself—that. li-e-gets far more than his proper share of the pro- fits of commerce. In fact, " the greedy, grasping middleman" has now become quite a familiar phrase. One of the latest of the middleman's. assailants has been trying to compute how much of the benefits ef the enhanced trade at Christmas has been intercepted between the producer and the consumer. We are told that it has been a real "Merry Christmas" for the taiddleman section of • the community. Whilst the farmers on one hand are grumbling about low prices, and tLe unem- ployed, on the other hand, are demonstrating their poverty, the middleman has been" standing on velvet." Take the case of bread, says the critic. Wheat is cheaper now than it has been for two hundred years, yet he contends that the householder who buys his bread is deriving very little benefit from the cheapness,. while there is a profit of nearly one hundred per cent. pocketed somewhere between the wheat- grower and the bread-eater. Then as to the Christmas beef, the same cantankerous critic treats all and sundry to elaborate statistics, with the object of showing the difference in the price of Christmas beef as it is sold wholesale and as it is retailed over the butcher's counter. He even goes so far as to insinuate that what is labelled as "prime English beef" has oftentimes been reared and fattened on Canadian homesteads or upon the prairie ranches of Wyoming or Texas; and that., the "prime Scotch 'S and "prime Welsh " mutton has really made the ocean passage from New- Zealand or the River Plate in a modern refrigera- tor. In spite of all these criticisms, however, the middleman continues to hold his own with a tena- city that shows he cannot be done without. It is just possible that his lot is not always one of such unbroken prosperity as some people seem to imagine. • It is noticeable that those who are fond of comparing wholesale with retail prices often apparently forget that there are such things as • rents and rates and taxes and incidental losses which the much-maligned " middleman " has to face. These things make a difference. At the same time, there is a natural presumption that , when wheat is cheap, bread should be cheap, and that there should be some correspondence be- tween the wholesale and the retail priCes of meat.

THE Christmas of 1894 has been darkened by tragic incidents. In-

Christtnas Casualties.

the now long list of railway acci- dents there are few of a more painful character than that which occurred in Chelford station on Saturday last. One of those marvellously rapid expiess trains which run to and from London was , there :suddenly wrecked whilst at its fullest speed, with results that threw a shadow over the festivities of the sca,son. The train was heavily freighted with passengers when it left Ma-riches- ter, and under the circumstances it is perhaps not a little remarkable that the list of killed and very seriously injured did not assume even more de- plorable proportions. As it is, however, the thir

===== 0002601_18941229_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
teen killed and fifty injured make a roll of victims which is sufficiently formidable in all conscience. There will no doubt be a rigorous inquiry into the causes of the accident. Such an inquiry is due in the interests of the nation generally and in justice to the bereaved. In the end, the blame—if blame attach to any human being—will no doubt be rightfully appor- tioned,and some lessons for guidance in the future sheuld be derived from the calamity. Unfor- tunately, in spite of all the precautions that the •experience of the, past has suggested, it seems to be impossible to entirely eliminate risks for rail- way travelling. Those risks are, it must be ad- mitted, when measured by the enormous number of railway travellers, really on a very small scale, but that is no reason why they should not be still further reduced if possible. Such a result seems to be the only off-set for disasters like that of Saturday, and it is but a sorry compensation fo r those who have been stricken or bereaved. Un- fortunately, the railway accident does not by any means exhaust the list of Christmas casualities. The storm which raged on Friday night and Satur- day seems to have wrought mischief to life and property in all parts of the Kingdom. Even yet the whole tale of misfortune has not been told. Alike on sea and land the destruction has been enormous, and it is computed that some hundreds of lives have been lost. As for the damage to property of one kind and another it must be almost incalculable.

A PATHETIC story was made A Pathetic Case. public at the inquest held on Saturday, at Runcorn, with regard to the death of a workman named RICHARD -JONES. JONES was up till seven months ago an industrious and steady workman, but at that time, apparently through no fault of his own, he was thrown out of work. The earch for fresh employment was fruitless, but still his wife and five children had to be maintained. With unselfish devotion the husband and fat her persisted in starving rather than that they should go,short and he appears to have almost wholly existed for some time past on the scraps of food and stimulants which were given him in payment for odd jobs that he could pick up. Life could not long be sustained on this aliment, and the poor man suddenly, a 3 it were, ceased to exist on the Friday of last week. Cases of this kind must be a rude shock to those persons who maintain that there is always work for willing hands, and that sheer want is always a result of idleness, thrift- lessness, or vice.

WARRINGTON BOARD pF QUA RDIANS.

--- - The last meeting of the old Board of Guardians was .held at the Workhouse, on Monday morning. Mr. W. Houghton presided, and there were also present Messrs. R. Heaton,A. J. Jolley, S. Davies, T. Mather, W.Turton, .G. Pilling, A. Jolley, Swinton, Clarke, Dr. Patterson, J. Dignum, W. Gill, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (deputy clerk),C. H. Ward (master), H. M. Mines (sup- erintendent). COST OF TUB ELECTIONS.

Mr. HEAToN asked what was the cost of the recent elections.—The CLERK said that he had not yet settled all accounts, but he had seen one union where the Clerk was reckoning it to cost £6so.—Mr. HEATON : We generally do it at half price here. (Laughter.)— The CLERK said that the next election would not cost so much as the last one, as he would have all accounts kept for reference. MR. BALHARRY'S SERVICES.

A resolution passed by the Newton Relief Committee en the motion of Mr. G. Ratcliffe, seconded by Mr. Hibbert, was read, in which a vote of thanks was .passed to Mr. Balharry for his long and faithful ser- -vices as chairman of the committee.—The CHAIRMAN said that he thought that Mr. Balharry's services had been so signal that he fully deserved that recognition. Be would like to know how long Mr. Balharry had been on the Board.—The CLERK said that it was over a quar- ter of a century.—The CHAIRMAN said that during his ,own time on the Board of twelve years, he had travelled over 5,000 miles in coming to and from the Board, and he was curious to know how far Mr. Balharry had travelled as he had been more than twice as long on the Board,and he lived twice as far. He mentioned that as showing how much more travelling the country members had than the town members.

INCREASE OF VAGRANCY.

A letter was read from the clerk of tho County Council as follows: " Dear sir,—The following resolution passed by the Herefordshire County Council and the Hereford- shire Quarter Sessions, was brought before the Standing Joint Committee for police and other purposes at their last meeting: Resolved that in view of the most alarm- ing increase of vagrancy throughout England, the Council urges on the Local Government Board, the necessity of taking energetic steps to abate the public nuisance thereby occasioned, and sugrsts to them the propriety of conferring with representatives of Boards of Guardians and Wayfarers' Relief Societies throughout the country, as to the best course to be pursued with this object,' when the committee resolved, 'that the Clerk be instructed to apply to the Clerks to the Guar- dians asking to be furnished with a return showing the number of vagrants in their respective Unions dwing the three years previous to the 31st Oct., 1894.' I shall be glad if you will kindly lot me have the particulars asked for at your earliest convenience —YourQ,etc.,F.C. Huvrow." A MISSING RUNCORN ENGINEER.

Mr. Frederick John Le Couteur, engineer on the steam tug "Liberator." son of Captain Le Couteur, of Trentham-st., Runcorn, has been missing since Wednesday morning. At an early hour on that day he was left by a friend near the Parish Church, Runcorn, when he expressed his determination to go to the vessel which was lying in the Ship Canal, at the Duke's pierhead, Weston Point. A fear is entertained that he may have fallen into the waterway and been, drowned. Grappling operations have been commenced. ST PAUL'S BIBLE CLASS.—CIIRISTMAS SERVICE. THE t..-:.; XL AND THE EASTERN TRAFFIC.

The Titan, one of Mr. Alfred Holt' s (Ocean Steamship Company) line will leave London on the sth prox., light, for the port. of Manchester. She will at once load for China and Japan. and if expectations are realised will load so fully that there will be no call at either Birken- head or Liverpool. She will sail directly from the port of Manchester either to Shanghai or Yokohama. One of the permanent pu-ts of the company has visited Man- chester to-day on the subject, and before bringing the Titan up the canal will take an experimental voyage from Liverpool to Manchester. The Titan is due in Manchester on the Bth January. and it is expected that the service thus inaugurated will rapidly develop. The present arrangements are for a sailing every three weeks

THE EXAMIN ER sA.TURDxII, DECEMBER 29, 1894.

THE GREAT STORM-. IMMENSE DAMAGE. DREADFUL RAILWAY ACCIDENT. A disastrous railway accident happened on Saturday evening at Chelford, on the system of the London and North-Western Company. The express from Manchester to London left London-aoad Station at a quarter past four with between four and five hundred passengers. On aprnaching Chelford the speed of the train was over fifty miles an hour. Just before that station was reached an empty waggon standing on the down line was blown by the high wind towards the points of a siding. Here it collided with a shunting engine and fouled the up line. The London express was caught by the obstruc- tion and was wrecked within the station. Fourteen passengers were killed, and a large number—probably over fifty—injured very seriously. Many of the sufferers lost their limbs. With as little delay as possible those passengers whose injuries were of a serious nature were taken to the hospitals at Crewe and Stockpert. Major Marindin visited Chelford on Wednesday, and on benalf of the Board of Trade made a careful examina- tion of the scene of the railway disaster. Subsequently he opened an inquiry at Crewe,at which Mr.Bryce,M.P., President of the Board of Trade,was present. 'Evidence was given as to the circumstances of the accident the Chef ford stationmaster. the men who were in the shunting of the goods train, the driver and fire- man of the second of the express envities, and others. There was a general agreement that Lue danger arose so suddenly that practically no Wal ~.,eAcoul:-4 he given to the express before it dashed, at full speed into a pro- jecting waggon. Some of the onlookers were net even aware for a few moments that a collision had taken place. The driver ,of the goods train thought at first that the stationmaster and a brakesman had been run over, and that that was why the express had pulled up so suddenly. None of the :nen engaged in the shunting operations heard any noise of a collision. The driver of the express second engine stated, on the other hand, th,..t the shock was so violent that it could not have been caused by a side blow, but must have been the result of an end-long collision.— Samuel Scott, the front van guard, saw the red lamp waved by the stationmaster as, far away almost as the distance signal, but he did not realise till too late that it was a warning not to the goods train but to the express itself. The stationmaster said he was responsible for the shunting operations, which were carried out in the usual way, and without any violent bumping. His attention having been called to a rule providing that where detached waagens are being shunted they should be in charge of a brakesma,n or a man with aprags, he explained that in his opinion this rule only applied where there was a probable danger of the waggons coming into violent contact. The method pursued at Chelford was the one in practice all over the' line. The injuiry was adjourned. 'The fearful gale which raged on Friday night and Saturday did much damage in all parts of the country, and unfortunately caused great loss of life. At the mouth of the Mersey the storm was fearful. A schooner was wrecked and four lives lost. The lifeboat men did gallant service during the day. In the city the gale did great damage to property. The ferry terrffic to New Brighton and Egremont was suspended. At Douglas great damage was done to the exhibition buildings at Belle Vue. A trawler drifted out of the harbour. and . there is anxiety as to her safety. The storm raged fiercely at Blacklaool, causing havoc to property and placing many men at sea in danger. The lifeboat crew went out twice, the first time to the Fleetwood trawler Petrel, and the second to the Norwegian- barge Abana. Both boats were driven ashore, one near Claremont Park, and the other at Norbreck, and twenty-two sea- men were rescued. The St. Annes lifeboat crew were oat for many hours, and did good service. Two vessels went ashore at Southport. On Saturday morn- ng three vessels—the Kirk Michael, from Liverpool to Melbourne; the Valhalla, from Liverpool 'to Vera Cruz ;and the Titania—went ashore off Holyhead. The crews of two vessels were rescued by the lifeboat man. , The boats, however, failed to reach the Kirk Michael, and the life-saving aparatus was brought out. Twelve of the crew were saved, and seven are missing. All through Lancashire and Yorkshire the effects of the gale were disastrous. At Preston a portion of the Manchester • Mill was demolished, and Hugh Haydgick, a weaver.was killed. Several other persons were injured. The ' Blackburn and East Lancashire Infirmary was partially wrecked, and four patients buried in the fallen masonry. They were rescued, but at great peril, the rescue party I being precipitated through the broken flooring. Mr. Josiah Greaves, formerly a member of the Blackburn ' Town Council, was kiiled by a gate which was blown upon him by the gale. A tramcar was blown over at Accrington, and much damage done to property. A • lofty chimney fell at Haslingden and killed a young man. A chimney fell at Burnley and injured four men. At Bradford a house was wrecked and a woman , crushed to death. Two persons in the same town were injured by a tramcar which was capsized by the wind. • There were many other accidents, and altogether twelve ' persons were hurt. A tall chimney fell at the works of Messrs. W. Cooke and Co., at Tinsley, near Sheffield, killing three men and two boys. A chimney at the chemical works at, Woodhouse Carr, Leeds, was blown across a house, burying a woman and six children. One child was taken lifeless from the wrecked dwelling. In Manchester a woman was killed, many persons were injured,and there was great damage to property.

DAMAGE AT RUNCORN.-WRECK OF VESSELS.

A fearful gale broke over Runcorn on Friday night and Saturday morning, and much damage was done throughout the town. The roofs of a number of build- Ines were partially removed, and chimney pots and slates were dislodged without number. Although not accompanied with loss of life, there Were several very narrow escapes. The proprietor of Wilson's Hotel (Mr. Charles Dutton) whilst in one of the rooms in which there was a skylight, a chimney pot came crashing through the class, and he was covered with the falling cithris. Fortunately the only injuries he received were a few snperficial cuts en the face and hands. A man named Robinson, of Penketh-lane, one of the servants of the local authority, sustained a very serious cut on the head by a falling slate. He was working opposite the shop of Mr. Gregory, Bridge-street, when he was struck. He was carried into the establishment of the executors of the late Mr. Isaac SpeAkman, chemist where he was attended by Dr. Ormsby, who was pass- ing. This gentleman fcund that the wound was three inches in length, and of a deep character. One of the Runcorn postmen was struck by a falling slate, and had his band cut. A lad, whilst passing in front of Mr. Lightfoot's shop, in High-street. was struck on the foot, another lad whilst crossing Church-street fell, and a large slate fell flat on his back whilst he wag laid on the ground. The boy was cut slightly on the neck by the slate breaking. Considerable damage was done to the roofs of the National school, Church-street ; Primitive Methodist Chapel, Greenway-road ; and the Soapery works of the United Alkali Compank. The tower at Mr. Abel's house in Greenway-road, sustained great damage and it was thought at the time that it would collapse altogether. The Parish Church, the Highfield Tannery, one of the mortuary chapels at the Ceme- tery, and a number of private houses were also damaged. The river Mersey was very turbulent, and the water was very high. In some parts it was nearly level with the wall dividing the river from the Ship Canal, and it was feared that the structure might be injured, but the works have stood the severe test. As was expected many vessels from Runcorn met with mishaps during the gale. The flat Carrie, belonging to Mr. R. Abel. Runcorn, laien with rails. sank abont 500 yards from the lightship opposite Gar;ton locks. The crew managed to get into the boat and landed at Speke. A flat nPmed Annie, the property ot Mr. S. Taylor J.P., of Runcorn, was being laden out of the schooner Sappho, anchored some 500 yards from the Flash buoy, Garston, when she foundered. Captain Edward Ash- croft and the mate, both of Runcorn, boarded the schooner and thus saved their lives. The vessel was taking in a cargo of china clay for Warrington. The flat John, belonging to Messrs. Richard Evans and Com- pany, and chartered by the United Alkali Company, was sunk off Speke. The crew. Edward Summerfield and James Northcote, were in the rigging from 5 a.m. until 11. a.m. The captain of tha John and William, Widnes (James Summerfield), his mate (Samuel Atherton and Frank Southern, both of Widnes), and Mr. John Davies, ef Runcorn, went and rescued them from their perilous position. They were in an exhausted condition. "Lion," a flat beloneing to Messrs. P. Speakman and Sons, Runcorn, had started away light, but through the storm was forced back into the *Alexandra jetty, Liver- pool, where it sank on Saturday morning. The captain, Thomas Williams, and the hand, Edward Bond, both of Widnes, jumped from the rigging OR to the granite quay, and were injured. TilP latter was so seriously hurt that he had to be removed on the ambulance to Bootle Hospi- tal. His forehead was badly cut. The captain also sustained injuries. The flat, although an exceedingly strong one, was smashed to pieces.—Another vessel, a schooner, the James and Mary, bound from the West of England for Runcorn, with a cargo of china clay, went ashore a little south of Southport shortly before noon. The lifeboat crew left the sailing boat and went out in the rowing boat, and took off the captain and four men forming the crew. They reached Southport just after four, and were cared for by the officials of the Ship- wrecked Mariners' Society.

I.adly shaken. In Newton a large pear troe which has weathered the storms of many years, was uprooted, and blown to the ground. In several cases fences were torn up and hurled some distance away, and pedestrians had , narrow escapes from falling slates.

THE GALE AT EARLESTOWN.

The storm which razed over the country during the early hours of Saturday morning. visited Earlestown and district with tremendous severity, and much damage was done. Fortunately, however, there were no fatali- ties, and with one exception the inhabitants escaped without injury. Numerous hoardings, chimney pots and slates were scattered about, as evidence of the destruc- tive character of the gale. In Er le-t., a shop window was blown bodily in and destroyed, the contents consist- logo? groceries being much damaged. In Bridge-st., a large sign over the shop of Mr. T. Rudd was observed to be in a dangerous condition on Saturday morning, and a young fellow named Eachus was endeavouring to secure it. Whilst doing so, an exceptionally strong gust of wind caught the sign, and hurled it and the ladder on which the youth was standing, to the ground. Tlnfor- tunately Eachus fell under the sign, and when rescued was found to be unconscious. He was removed home on the ambulance litter, and it was found that he was

===== 0002601_18941229_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRIMiION.

The storm blew with great violence in Warring ton during Friday night and Saturday. Alth-ugh there had been a great number of minor accidents, there have not been any which may be classed as serious. Slates and chimney pots were flying about in all directions, and numerous narrow escapes are reported. In one case, past of a chimney fell through the roof of a house on to a bed which a person had just left with such force as to send the feet of the bed through the floor. In Winwick-road almost the whole of the tiles on the roof of a row of houses were

blown off. In the row of houses near to the Coach and Horses in Liverpool-road, some of the tiles were lifted off the roof and carried across the road on to the railway, where they were smashed. Numerous windows were either blown in, or smashed. A large win- dow belonging to Mr. Dolan, clothier, Horvemarket-st., was broken during Saturday morning by a chimney pot whirl' fell from the next shop. The weather vane of Ihe Town Cluck was also in a very insecure state, and from Sankey-st., it could plainly be seen shaking violently at every blast. Fortunately it did not give way and fall into the street. In Cheshrres-lane, the wind completely demolished a brick wall, awl a child had an exttemely narrow escape at the time, having only just passed before the wall fell. Mr. Place, the market in-pector, had also a narrow escape, for the wind set a lurry in motion near to him, and he just managed to get out of the way in conse- quence of the shouts of the bystanders. There does not appear to have been any extensive damage done in the works in the town although a quantity of woodwork was blown down in Messrs. Crosfield's.

Of the row of fine trees fronting Mrs.Broadbent's house at Latchford,: there are only five left—two at one cor- ner and three at the other. During the gale no less than three of these splendid trees were blown down, and what makes the loss more aparent is the fact that they are all from the centre of the row, and adjoining one another. For the last few years these "veterans " have been coming dawn with disappointing regularity, as almost every storm of recent years has elaimed its victim from amongst them. When the storm was at its highest, several new features were noticeable in connection with the Ship Canal. A high tide in addition to the wind, raised the water level an extra 10 feet, while above the locks where the Canal is tideless, it was almost as high, and for most of Saturday morning was flowing uninterruptedly over the tops of the gates. Waves three feet high and break- ing with foam were also much in evidence, and the wind in the immediate neighbourhood was unmistakably salt to the taste. Gulls, too, were occasionally to be seen dashing al-out after bits of floating refuse. Naturally the force of the wind prevented any vessels whatever from attempting the passage between the two ports. The R.C. Church of the Sacred Heart, in Liverpool- road, which was almost finished, was discovered to be in a dangerous condition, early on Saturday morning. The upper portion ot the tower showed signs of giving way,tand orders were at once given to stop vehicular traffic in Liverpool-road for about thirty yards. Ropes were placed across the road, and a policeman was put on duty at either rape to stop everyone from passing. Con- sequently all 'buses, carts, and pedestrians who wished to go from Bank Quay to the market, or vice versa, were compelled to go round by Milner-st. and Priestly-- et. But, although the upper portion of the tower was considerably shaken by the wind, nothing gave way,and contrary to the expectations of a great many people, the tower is still standing. It is expected however that a portion will have to be taken down in order to renuild it.

FEARFUL SUFFERINGS OF WARRINGTON MEN During the storm on Saturday morning the flat" John," belonging, to Messrs. Richard Evans and Co., but chartered by the United Alkali Company, Widnes, and laden with copper, was badly damaged, and the crew consisting of two men were picked up in an exhausted. condition, after having been in the rigging over five hours. It appears that the flat, which was in charge of the captain, Edward Summerfield, and his mate, James Nornott, both of Sankey Bridges, was lying at anchor between Speke and Hale Head when the storm com- menced about two o'clock on Saturday morning. About seven o'clock, in consequence •of the flat labouring heavily, the captain sent his mate to let out more chain. On going to slo this he found that the fore hatches had burst, and that the waves were making a clean breach into the vessel. The captain at once knocked the pin out of a shackle in the chain to the anchor, and had a jib sail hoisted, and ran the flat ashore, thus saving the valuable cargo of copper with which it was laden. Still the men were in no better position.for the tide was high, and they could not land. They then took to the rigging, where they remained until the tide ran out when they dropped on to the beach in an exhausted condition. The fiat" John and William," belonging to the United Alkali Company, had also blown ashore about half a mile further on, and the captain, James Summerfield, brother to the captain of the flat John, and the crew, Samuel Atherton and Frank Davies, all of Sankey Bridges, were able to reach dry ground. They at once went to Widnes, and reported the condition the flat was in. On returning to the flat, in company with John Davies, the foreman earpenter of the Alkali Company, they were met by a man who told them of the two men being in distress. They at once went and found both the men on the ground in an unconscious condition. A gamekeeper named White procured some spirits, and after some time the men were brought round. They were at once sent home, and we are glad to state that they are fast recovering after their trying experience. On Sunday a packet and a number of men were sent to the flat John, and succeeded, after taking most of the cargo off, in bringing the flat to Wooden& The vessel is in a very battered condition. THE PANTOMIME AT ST. HELENS. DICK WHI ITINGTON."

The anticipations or a sprendid success by the pro- duction of the pantomime of " Dick Whittington "at the handsome new Theatre Royal and Opera House, Corporation-street, St. Helens, have been realised to the full. The genial and pnpolsr proprietor, Mr. Wallace Revill, has succeeded in eclipsing all his previous efforts in that direction and he has more than merited the warm congratulations he has received from his many friends in the town on his brilliant achievement in providing his patrons with an almost faultless performance which is in every way happily appropriated to the festive season. - The company inaludee some of the best artistes in the pantomimic lime now in the country and it is generally considered to be the best that has ever been gathered together In St. Helens on a similar occasion. The title role is assumed by Miss Emily Lyndale a lady full of dash and go, from the principal halls in London. Last year she was the principal " boy " in the pantomime at the Theatre Royal. Leicester, where she achieved a great success. Her efforts are ably seconded by Miss Lizzie Boone as "Alice," who previously appeared in St. Helens in the character of " Tina " in "My Sweetheart," and she lends no small amount of brightness and vivacity to the per- formance. The other ladies include Miss Gertrude Craven, Miss Harriet Glenrnse, Miss Carrie Barnes, Miss Daisy Stanton, and Miss Daisy Norman, all of whom have previonsly figured with considerable success in pantomime work. The Brothers Passmore, from the Theatre Royal, Sheffield. have a high reputation as pan- tomimiats, clever vocalists,dancers and knockabouts ,and fully sustain their characters. Mr. Fred May is a mem- ber of the celebrated May family and is a capable artiste Mr. James La Fane is also a well known pantomimist. while Messrs. Maurice and Ross are smart knockabout artistes. Mr. A. P. Turner is another pantomimist of more than ordinary ability. Sixty local children take part in the production, and new eancee and ballets have been arranged for them by Madame E. Basaano, ably assisted by Miss Carrie Barnet'. Magnificent new scenery has been painted by Mr. Keith and his asaintants.and the principal scenes are the Goblin Belfry of Bow Bells, Old Cheapside, Fitz- warren's Countine-hnuee.Highgate Hill, Near the Docks, Port of London. Dominion of Emperor Turn Turn the Terrible. The Royal Palace, where there is a grand fete and Oriental ballet, and a variety entertainment ; Dear Old Albion, and the Guidhall. Old Cheapside, Highgate Hill, Port of London, and the Royal Palace, are con- sidered to be real works of art in the scene-painter's line, and come in fora large share of admiration. In the scene," Port of London," there is a grand mechanical change, showing the wreck and loam of the "Saucy Polly." A brilliant feature of the production is the mag- nificence and splendour of the dresses and costumes which have been made under the personal direction of Mrs. Revill, and are rich, tasteful and pretty in their harmonious blending of colour. The songs and music which have been arranged by Messrs. Carl Hamlin and F. W. Dearlove, are bright and up to date, and there appears to be no ,reason why " Dick Whittington " should not have a most successful run during the holiday season. The opening night was last S.turday, when notwith- standing the very miserable atmospherical conditions a large audience assembled and the performance was en- thusiastically welcomed. The entire piece ran with re- markable smoothness, and the humourous features were demonstratively appreciated. The entertainment is also a brilliant success as a spectural display. Amid a wealth of beautiful scenery Old Cheapside, Highgate Hill, and the Royal Palace are gems of scenic work, the palace Scene being really gorgeous and magnificent. It is in this scene that one of the prettiest effects of the pan- tomime is produced when representatives of all nations, attired in national dress, march in with their respective banners. The transformation scene, "Cupid's Paradise," is also a magnificent piece of artistic scene painting and all the efforts are extremely well arranged. The barlequinade has been arranged by Mr. Harry Wys, who lieures as the clown,and he is ably assisted by Mr. Alfred Maurice as pantaloon, Mr. James Ross as the policeman, Mr. Alfred rassmore as harlequin, and Miss Carrie Barnes as columbine. The properties and all the appara- tus necessary are of the most complete character in all respects, as they have been supplied by the most %ell- known firms, and everything augurs brightly for a sua , of brilliant success.

CHRISTMAS TIDE AT RUNCORN.

Christmas Day passed off very quietly in Runcorn. The weather was damp and foggy, a drizzling rain setting in which militated against outdoor enjoyment. There were the usual Christmas services at churches and chwels. At Dutton Workhouse, the inmates, were supplie,l with the usual Christmas fare of roast beef and plum pudding.

MUSICAL PERFORMANCE ON CFIRIS tikIAS EVE.

In the Public i:all, en Christmas Eve, the Runcorn Tonic-Solfa Prize Choir gave an effective rendering of the sublime oratorio "Messiah." There was a fairly- large audience, who greatly appreciated the perform- ance. The choir, under the leadership of Mr. John Holford, rendered the choruses in a very efficient man- ner, especially the "Hallelujah Chorus," into which they entered with spirit. The orchestral band under the conductorship of Mr. Collision, played the various parts in an extremely able manner. The principal voca- lists were Madame :Jeannie Sadler (soprano), Mr Riley Walker (tenor), Mr. Joseph Layland (bass), and Miss C. Taylor, of Runcorn. "Behold a Virgin," was Miss Tay- lor' it first effort, but she appeared to most advantage in the solo, " He was despised and rejected," for which she received a well-merited encore. Madame Sadler at once established herself as the star of the evening by her in- terpretation of the solo, " There were shepherds," and in that most difficult number, " 11,.joice greatly," Madame Sadler excelled. Mr. Riley Walker was also very successful in the solos allotted to him, and the same may be said of Mr. Joseph Layland's singing. On several occasions b )th artistes were encored. Mr. W. Cooke ably accompaniel on the organ. THE CHRISTMAS RELIGIOUS SERVICES. The customary special services on Christmas day were held in Runcorn churches and chapels, and on the whole, the attendance was very good. Many of the places of worship had been appropriately decorated for the occa- si.m with evergreens. At the Parish Church the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood (vicar) preached at the morning service, and in the evening a carol service was held. A number of Christmas carols was sung in a praiseworthy manner by the choir. The soloists were Miss Lake, Mr. Branch and Mr. Garner. There was a large congrega- tion. Services were also held in the morning at St. Michael's and Holy Trinity Churches, and also at the Seamen's Mission. There was celebration of mass at the St. Edwards Roman Catholic Church on Christmas morning, when there was a large congregation. At the St. Paul's Wesleyan Church, the Rev. T. Brackenbury conducted a service in the morning, and the Roy. J. H. Tite acted in a similar capacity at Halton Wesley an Church.—Special Christmas carol services were held on Sunday in the St. John's Presbyterian Church and the Primitive Methodist Chapel, Greenway-road.

===== 0002601_18941229_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
TREATS TO POOR CHILDREN.

Through the generosity of the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi and several local gentlemen a substantial dinner was pro- vided for 200 poor children in the town on Christmas Day at St. Edward's schools. The dinner consisted of hot-pot of excellent quality, and it was, as a matter of course, was done full justice to. The Revs. Dr. Gastaldi and Jerron, and Dr. Mangan visited the schools during the dinner, • and they were loudly cheereA by the youngsters. The following attended to the wants of the children :—Misses Agnes Clinch, Annie Clinch, Doran and Dolan ; Messrs. James Byrne, John Morris, R. Clinch, J. P. Quinn, Robert Dolan, and John Ryan. The general arrangements were made by Mr. James Percival. —On Wednesday evening, by subscriptions collected by the members of the Greenway-road Primitive Metho- dist Christian Endeavour Class, and also by the "Do Good Brethren," an excellent tea was provided for poor children in the Primitive Methodist Schoolroom. The number of children supplied with tea exceeded the anticipations of the promoters, no fewer than 770 sitting down. The wants of the youngsters were well looked after by a large body of workers ef the Christian Endea- vour Class. After the tables had been cleared a magic lantern entertainment was given by Mr. Bosworth, the proceedings being presided over by Mr. T. Reynolds. The pictures shown on the screen were illustrative of the life of Christ, and there were also exhibited several well- known views of places in and around Runcorn, pictures of public men in Runcorn, concluding with a number of comic sketches. The programme was agreeably diversi- fied by the rendition of songs, readings and recitations by the following: Miss Vernon, Mr. R. Illidge, Mr. T. Johnson, Mr. J. Stubbs, and Mr. Richardson. Each child was presented with an orange on leaving the building.

DUTTON WORKHOUSE.

Christmas Day at the Dutton Workhouse was spent in the usual pleasant way. The day's proceedings com- menced with morning service in the chapel, which had been prettily decorated with various mottoes, shields, and evergreens. The pulpit looked very neat, being covered with ivy and golden fir, with a blue centre and letters of goli inscribed thereon. Carols were sung in place of the usual hymns, and the whole of the musical portion of the service was well rendered by the choir. The nsatron (Mrs. Cunnah) presided at the organ. The Chaplain (Rev. L. D. Lyon Bennett) preached an appropriate sermon and took his text from St. Luke xi. 5. Mrs. Marsden, of Aston, kindly sent for each child a bag containing sweets, eranges, nuts and cakes, which were distributed after the service. Dinner was served in the dining hall, which had been decorated with various pictures, mottoes, holly, Chinese lanterns, &c. The fare for dinner consisted of roast beef, pota- toes, and plum pudding. Milk was provided for the children to drink, and beer for those .4 the older in- mates who preferred it. In the eveninga miscellaneous cenccrt which had been arranged by the Master (Mr Cunnah), was given in the dining hall, where a sub_ stantial platform had been erected. A dialogue by four of the girls," Should girls help their mother," was capit. ally render,d, and a well-known inmate—George Thoinp-on—crea.ted much pleasure by his playing on the violin. Mr. H. Allison (the School Master) caused plenty of fun with his comic songs. Three hearty cheers for the Guardians and,the singing of the National Anthem brought what had been a pleasant day to a close. The following have sent gifts to the inmates :—Mrs. Mars- den, Mrs. Wood, Miss Best, Mrs. Whitley, Mrs. F. Nor- man, Mrs. Rodtrers (Newton B ink), Lady Greenall, Mr. H. Talbot, Dr. Robinson (Runcorn), and Mrs. C. Rey- nolds (Frodsham). THE WORK AT THE POST OFFICE._

The officials at the Runcorn Post Office experienced a very busy time this Christmas. About double the num- ber of letters and parcels were dealt with on Monday, as compared with the same date a year ago. Oa Christ- mas Eve the number of letters posted at Runcorn was enormous, which seemed to point to the fact that the public had refrained from purchasing their Chrismas greetings until Monday. The mails received on Christmas morning were very heavy,and the work taxed the energies of the officials to the utmost. But Mr.Orrell and his assistaats are to be congratulated in having com- pleted their duty without causing much delay.

SAD DEATH AT RUNCORN. "DIED FROM DRINK AND WANT OF FOOD."

At the Tine Hotel, Runcern. on Saturday, Mr. J. E. Worelev, coroner, held an inquest on the body of Richard Jones, 37 years of age, of 58, Shaw-st., who died sud- denly on Friday. The first witness called was Mary Tlaneah Jones, who stated that the deceased was her husband. Ile had en- joyed good health but was addicted to drink, and when taking it was unable to eat anything. On Monday he was seized with cramp ia his ankles, but afterwards recovered, and continued to be well until Friday after- noon, at two o'clock, when he became ill. He expired within an hour, and before the arrival of the doctor. Elizabeth Rawlinson, sister to the previous witness, gave corroborative evidence. She stated that deceased had been out of work seven months, although he had tried to obtain it. He had scarcely taken any food lately, being without it four days at a time. He refused it in order that his five children might not go short. His wife and witness had tried to keep the house going by washing, &c. P.C. Gregory said Dr. McDougall saw Jones, but through professional engagements was unable to be present at the inquiry. The medical gentleman had told witness that it was a bad case. and that drink and want ef food had caused death. On going te the house he found food therein. Deceased had evidently starved himself for his children. The officer had known him for three years, and up to seven months ago he was a very hard working man at Weston works. He did not drink to excess at that time. His wife had stated that he would not taste food. He had obtained drink by doing odd jobs for persons. The Coron4r, in summing up, said the case was one of a man who had a wife, sister-in-law, and five children, and who, up to seven months ago, was a hard working, industrious man. Then he was thnwn out of employment, and since the family had to be supported by the wife, augmented by the small earnings from the sister-in-law. Deceased seemed to have done his best to obtain work, but failed. He, however, got odd jobs, for the performance of which he was paid in drink, and upon that drink and other assistance he appeared to have subsisted. From the evidence of Miss Rtwlinson he had actually denied himself food in erder.that his wife and family might have a large share. From the doctor's statement there was no doubt that death was due to drink and want of food. It was a very sad case indeed—it was one in which a man had sacrificed him- self for the benefit of his children. He was out of work through no fault of his own. A verdict of " Died from drink and want of food " was recorded. The jurors, in order to render assistance to the bereaved ones, gave the widow their fees, which was supplemented by the Coroner, who suggested that the case should be brought under the notice of any re- lief organizstion that might be in existence in the town. •

RIT6CORN BOARD OF OUA.RDIA.NS.

The last meeting of the Board was held at the Dutton Workhouse on Thursday afternoon. Mr. Mclntyre pre- sided, and there were also present the members of the S tnitary Authority, and Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P. S. Yarwood, C. Mounfield, A. R. Norman, T. Handley, J.P., E. Brathertoe, J. Ashton (clerk), and G. Ashton (deputy clerk). TUE REDEMPTION OF LOANS.

Mr. S. S. Provis, in reply to a letter of the clerk, said he was directed by the Local Government Bo rd to state that under the circumstances mentioned,the Board were not • repared to issue an order for the redemption, by the Guardians of the Runcorn Union, of the balances of loans of £2,400 and £6OO advanced to them by the Royal Exchange Assurance. Although the question was not free from doubt, the Board did not think that under Section 2of the Poor Law Loans Act, 1871, a Board of Guardians could require persons who had lent them money to take it Nack at a time earlier than that speci- fied in the mortgages.—lt was decided, on the motion of Mr. Heywood, to again write to the Local Government Board, stating that when the mortgage was drawn up the persons interested knew that it could be terminated by the sanction of the Local Government Bard. THE PROPOSED EXTENSION OF THE WARRINGTON BOROUGH BOUNDARY. _

The Clerk read the report of the committee appointed to consider the notice for the extension of the b•amclary of the borough of Warrington. The committee met at Warringtbn on the 19th December and there were present : Messrs. Joseph Knowles,. Jaihes Brandreth, T. Flandley, C. W. Davenport, Eyes, and Ashton (clerk). The letter served upon the Board of Guardians and the Sanitary Authority, and also the letter of the Local Government Board with reference thereto were read by the Clerk. He also submitted a summary of the area, population, number of houses, and rateable value of the portions of the Runcorn Union proposed to be trans- ferred to the borough, showing a total rateable value of £5,062. It was resolved that the Local Government Board be informed that the Board of Guardians and Rural Sanitary Authority objectrd to the proposed ex- tension, and consequent transfer. It was further resolved by the committee that the Clerk write to the overseers of the townships affected, and ask for the views of their respective Parish Councils in the Matter, and also to the Cheshire County Council, and London and North-Western Railway Company, and the Man- chester Ship Canal Company and inquire what they proposed to do. THE CLERK'S REMUNERATION.

Mr. Heywood said the Assessment Committee recom- mended that the sum of £25 be paid to the Clerk for extra services in regard to the county rate basis. That had been a great of extra work to the Clerk and he had managed to reduce the county rate basis to what would be a return to them of £2OO. Another branch of work, viz.: a return of all •the assessments of the Union, had caused a great deal of extra work to the Clerk and his assistants, and the committee recommended that he receive a further £25 for that, and also his usual salary of £63 15s. for nine months' service as Clerk t) the, Board. Mr. Handley seconded, and it was carried unanimously.

ANOTHER DEATH FROM BURNS AT

RUNCORN.

At the Duke of Devonshire Inn, Runcorn, on Thursday afternoon,Mr. J. E. Worsley held an inquest on the body of Marjory Rowles, 2 years and 9 months; daughter of Frederick Lewis Rowles, publican who died on the pre- vious morning from the result of burns sustained on Saturday. Mr. Rowles stated that on Sunday morning he awoke his daughter, Alecia, aged 16 years, who took down- stairs with her the deceased, who was followed by a four year old brother. Witness made up the fire on Saturday night so that it would not be necessary to light it in the morning. His daughter raked the ashes out, and then went into the yard for coal. Whilst she was away the deceased's night dress:became ignited. He could not say how this occurred. Witness heard screams came downstairs, and found his daughter in the yard, .; with deceased in a bucket of water, to put out the flames. A portion of the nightdress was still on fire and witness extinguished it. In his opinion, the head of a burning match fell into the child's lap, and set fire ' to the nightdress. Miss Alecia Rowles gave evidence in support of the above statement. She was not in a position to say how the accident happened. Dr. McKenzie said everything passible was done for the little girl from receiving the burns, up to the time of her death, which was due to convulsions, consequent on 44' the injuries. _

In humming up, the Coroner stated that it could not be too widely known that the very worst thing anyone could do, was to place a child which had been burnt into cold water. The very best thing was to take a woollen cloth, and fold it round the person, thus extinguishing the flames, and giving the person a better chance of recovery. He had no doubt that Miss Rowles did what ,

she thought best in acting as she did, and his remarks did not apply specially to her. What he said was for the benefit of the general public. A verdict of" Accidental death "was recorded.

CHRISTMAS AT WARRINGTON. PADGATE INDUSTRI&L_ SCHOOLS.

Christmas kept up at the Padgate Industrial Schools ""1 in the usual manner. A capital dinner, which consisted of roast beef, potatoes, and plum pudding, was served in the schoolroom, which had been tastefully decorated with palms and various kinds of plants, banners, Japan- ese lanterns, evergreens, Promptly at ene o'clock the bell was rung as the signal for the children to as- semble, which was done in good order. Grace having been sung, the dinner was commenced, and was very much enjoyed by the children. After they had had their fill of beef and potatoes,they were served with plum pudding. While the tables were being cleared for the serving of the pudding, the band of the schools played- the following selections of music, for the rendering of which they were heartily cheered :—" Hail, smiling morn," " 11. re's health tj, all good lasses," " The mis- tletoe bough, " F T he's a jlll7 good fellow," "Auld lang Sync," " t 1 m, sweet home," and "A fine old English gentleman." The following acted as carvers and waitresses Messrs. Mines, Aukland, Tickle, Wood, house, J. Fairhurst, and the Misses Mitchell,Hialehurst- Lea, and E. Mitchell. The cooking, which was done in an excellent manner, was under the superintence of the matron, Mrs. Mines. The Rev. J. J. Langhans was amongst the visitors present. After dinner Mr. MINES, the master, said this was the twelfth, Christmas they had spent at Padgate, and he did net think they had the slightest reason to regret anything that had passed during that twelve years. Through the generosity of the people of England, the poor of this country were not forgotton at this festive season. (Ap- plause.) No school children had sat down to a better dinner than they had that day. They had first of all to be thankful to the Great Giver of all good gifts, and then to the Guardians,who were responsible for their care and maintenance. He: was sorry the Chairman of the Guardians was not present, he was sure he had been un— avoidably detained. Be then called for three cheers for- the Guardians, which were given in right royal fashion. He afterwards called for three cheers for the chaplain, the Rev. J. J. Langham, Mr. Aukland and several lady friends which were given in the same hearty fashion. Mr. IaNGHAM then briefly addressed the children, and asked for cheers for Mr. Mines and all the other teaahers and officers of the institution, for the kindness they had shown and the trouble they had taken that day. The cheers were given in a heartier fashion than before, if possible, and the children joined in the singing of" For he's a jolly good fellow" and waving of handkerchiefs. Mr. MINES briefly replied on behalf of himself and the other officers, and informed those present that the Mayor (Councillor James Fairclough) had sent a case of oranges for the thildren, and Miss Kemp,Higher Walton, had also sent a bag of sweets and a packet of Christmas cards. Cheers having been given for the &mom of these presents, the children dispersed to enjoy them- selves in different games. Various amusements were carried on until about nine o'clock in the evening. AT THE INFIRMARY. At the Infirmary Christmas was observed as merrily as could be expected under the circumstances. The wards had all been tastefully decorated with seasonable - foliage, and everything had been made as comfortable for the sufferers as was possible. Presents of various kinds were received frem the Mayor and alayoress and Mrs. Monks for distribution, and the usual Christmas dinner was served to all. The aged men also received their customary allowance of tobacco. WESTON.

The prisoners then came near him and asked him to "treat them," to which he had no objection. They had beer twice at his expense and took payment from him. They afterwards had Some more ad for this complainant gave Simpson half a sovereign which she took to the landlady and got 93. 7d. change. RUGBY. WARRINGTON V. STOCKPORT.

These teams met on Saturday at Warring,ten, to play the return match. The result of the first meeting was a victory for Stockport by a goal and a try to nil, and as Stockport have only been teaten twice previously this season, a good match was looked forward to. The ex- pectations were rudely cast to the winds however on Saturday morning, for a tremendous gale was blowing, and was of such force that accurate play was impossible. The gale had only very slightly abated at the time for the kick off, and as a result, there was only a small gate, probably the smallest for a first team match, of the whole season. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 29, 1894.

his 25. Hem a pack was formed and Garrity passed over to Whitehead, who kicked, and the wind lifted the high and carried it over the bar, thus giving the Rangers a lead of four points. From the restart the home for- wards quickly got to work ,and carrying the pack rushed the bah over the Rangers' line, but Chapman kicked dead when one of the home players was in the act of falling on it. Even play followed, and the efforts of the players to gather the ball caused much amusement to the few enthusiasts who had braved the elements. At length, a cancerted rush ended in Walker scoring,but needless to say the kick was a failure, the wind carrying the ball across the ground. From the kick-out a move- ment was started which ended in Houghton grassing Berry in the home 25, the interval arriving with the score Runcorn, one try ; Rangers, one dropped goal. Squires restarted, and Chapman stayed a rush of the ' home forwards pluckily. but a moment later Warder had a drop at goal, which just passed outside. Runcorn now pressed severely, but much time was wasted by the ball being carried out of the ground, the wind at this juncture blowing with tremendous force. Conse- quently the play was of a desultory character, and wretched both for spectator and player alike. Time put an end to the fiasco, which was a relief to all. The final scere was—Runcorn, one try, four minors ; Rangers ,one dropped goal.

WARRINGTON v. WEST HARTLEPOOL.

For the holiday match at Nlarrington on Wednesday the homesters took on West Hartlepool, hitherto strangers to Warrington, who brought a representative team with them, including Gibbons, the North team forward. Some 3,000 spectators had assembled when Turner, half-an-hour after advertised time, kicked off. There was little to choose with regard to %.1 nd , for what wind there was was a cross-wind. The dulness of the day and the slipperiness of the ground was, however, a distinct drawback for good football. The play was not five minutes old when Nsvins, taking a pass from Tay- lor, scored in a favourite position, Burton putting on the finishing touch by placing a goal. West Hartlepool followed with a short turn of pressure, their essays,how- ever, being checked by Burton, who made a road for himself, and was well on the way when the whistle sounded for some informality. Armstrong transferred for a moment. This time Burton got clear away, and made a long and accurate pass to Barber, who held it well, but on repassing it to Moores, sent the ball out of the latter's reach. From the scrimmage which followed Barber made no mistake, securing neatly, and romping over an easy try, which, however, remained a try,Burton's kick for goal being charged down. Between a good run by Barber and a kick from his mark by Heesem, Warrineton kept close in the visitors' quarters, where a good defence was shown. Blakedale cleared with a hasty kick, which reached half-way. where Nevins stayed its further progress. The ball was speedily back again, causing Maclellan to kick dead when Barber was within an ace of scoring. Overton was the first to call upon Boscow, who exchanged with good interest, and let his tbree-qiiarters' get in their stride. By pretty combination between them, which non- plussed the visitors' defence, they put Garretty in the way to score, and this Garretty just managed to do, for he had difficulty in getting the better of Moralee. Gib- bons put in a powerful run to half-way, where Moores pluckily stopped his career. Half-time score :—War- rington, one goal two tries ; West Hartlepool, nil. On resuming the visitors showed there was still a lot of play left in them, for they assumed the aggressive, and Arm- strong narrowly missed landing a gaal from a well-taken mark. The home three-quarters, who showed so advan- tageously in the first halt, again got to work together, and Barber got well into his stride. He was closely at- tended to by Burton, and at the moment of being tackled he adroitly handed across to Burton, who had no difficulty in augmenting War- rington's score by another three points, Turner failing to increase their value. Gib- bons from a penalty put Warrington's goal in jeo- pardy, but the ball fell into the hands of Bascow, who had plenty of time to send it clear. The way was soon opened for Burton, and that speedy player making short ! work of the efforts of the visitors' full back, aeain scored. This try, so well earned, was poorly utilised by Turner. After a longer spell of defensive work than hitherto the Warrington three-quarter line came away with a racket, Burton in possession. When challenged, Burton simply passed to Heesom, who scored the sixth and last try for Warrington. Nevins's kick for goal failed. It was these frequent breakaways of the speedy Warrington wings that told against the visitors, who. though plainly overmatched, never acknowledged them- selves beaten, and, in fact, during the second half at least they had as much of the play as their opponents. Towards the close of the game they had the satisfeetinn of seeingMoralee,who in the second half was transferred from full back to three-quarter,break through the home defence and score in a favourable position. As Gibbons failed to improve the point, the final score read :—War- rington, 1 goal, 5 tries (20 points) ; West Hartlepoll, 1 try (three points).
