THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT WARRINGTON.

The Silver-street Wesleyan day school held a very successful 'concert on Thursday last week. Mr. Upson has had a very successful nine years at the school, and notwithstanding drawbacks owing to its situation, the school has produced some very good results. Mr. E. J. Guest, J.P , has worked very energetically on behalf of the school, and it is in a great measure due to him that the place has been carried on so well.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Savings Bank showed an excellent state of affairs, and if trade is bad in the town, the trade in the bank is increasing. The bank is,. from the statement of the actuary, on a very sub- stantial footing. It is the ambition of the chairman to have a sum of £300,000 in the bank. As there is already over £298,000 it is probable that his wish will soon be realized. The working people of the town are evidently tnrifty,taken as a whole, and it was stated that the best customers were among the small wage earners.

The good old custom of " roping " a wedding party has apparently not yet died a natural death, and the fact ot it being done at Stockton Heath recently led to a litigation at the County Court. The case was a rather amusing one. This particu- lar wedding party had been " roped " three times, and on coming to the fourth rope, the driver was determined not to submit to the blackmail, and in endeavouring to get past the rope he caused the downfall ot some pailings and damaged a stone coping. Mr. Bostock who owned the damaged property therefore took proceedings against the Cirriage Company who owned the vehicle. But there is such a thing a 4 a right of way, and as the driver was only exercising this right the verdict was in favour of the Carriage Company. This is hard upon the property owner no doubt but he will know better in what direction to look for redress the next time.

Warrington people are not quite as often treated to an interesting lecture on the evils of opium- smoking as they are to one on the social ravages committed by indulgence in intoxicating liquor, therefore more than usual interest was evinced in Mr. J. G. Alexander's lecture on his "Anti-opium Tour round the World" at the Y.M.C.A. on Wednes- d y night last.- In his address the lecturer instanced very strongly the bad effects, morally and physi- cally, of the use of opium as a "weed" 6r food. The work and purposes of the Royal Commission, which he accompanied,were also satisfactorily dealt with by him, but most of the interest was naturally centred in his account of an interview with Li Hung Chang, late prime minister of China, in which that statesman gave very decided opinions about the evils of the opium traffic, and the way in which England forced the Chinese to trade in it. The Y.M.C.A. "evenings " are now in full swing,and Mr. Stewart (secretary) is to be congratulated on the success and attractiveness which have, up to the preßent,characterized them.

Which cf the candidates in the Board of Guardians election was it that declared that he was "quite parsimonious" about the result? He meant, of course, that he was quite indifferent, but like Mrs. Malaprop he got hold of the wrong word. I am told that it was the same gentleman who, in discussing same municipal affairs, once declared with great emphasis, "The Town Council has no jurisprudencki in the matter but I acquee.ze." Who will say after this that there is no originality in Warrington?

I see that the new " Parish District and Town Councils' Guzette," deals in its legal column with the question, " Is discussion on the election of an alderman permissible ? " which has been warmly disputed in the Warrington Town Council. It states that it can and nothing in the statute to render discussion illegal. It therefore comes to the conclusion, which seems to be the merest common- sense, that the "absence of express permission cannot of itself be taken to amonnt to prohibition.' We are told as a matter of fact, that the Municipal Corporations' Act does not specially provide for dis- cussion-on any subject. It would appear from this that a discussion on any subject might be ruled out of order in the Town Council with just. as much legality as a discussion on the appointment of an alderman.

Monday :last week, !the night 'chosen for the performance of the concert in aid of the Cheshire Lines Committee Brass Band Fond ' was scarcely the most suitable, considering the important fact that it was election night. This no doubt accounted in some measure for the meagre attendancl at the concert, which was excellent from a tnuxical point of view. The two difficult and well played pieces by the band gave striking proof that, though not an old band by any means, they are capable of "tackling" I most of the ponolar pieces from the oper-ttas and overtur,s. The Liverpool and Manchester artistes, too, served to set. off well tlio talents of our lopal singers, judging from the encores and other manifestations of satisfaction, all succeeded in pleasng that fickle god—the Warrintzton public.

The crowded state of the streets at. the present time is no doubt largely due to the bright and at- tractive appearance of the shop windows which are decked out. with all kinds of finery, and Christmas. specialities. On looking through the four main streets, one finds no end of variegatel sights etq entertaining to the old as the young. No stronger proof of the imperative necessity for the widening of the streets near Market Gate could be wished for, then that provided by a walk—or rather an attempt at one— through. them R t the prezent season. At the first glance one almost despairs of getting through. without reslrtinz to the desperate resource of "climbing over heads." As thPre is an end to all things we may reasonably expect an end to this nuisance at tome future date and may-be—there's just a possibility—before the end of the present centuryl

As usual, when elections do not result to their satisfaction, our local Tories, or at. all events their newspaper. launch the amiable changes of " treachery" against the whole Liberal party. An agreement was, as every one now knows, come to that the Guardians elections should not be fought. politically, and in spite of all that has been or may he said, that. compact was faithfully observed by the Liberals. It is true that Mrs. Clay was strongly supported in Whitecross ward by many well-known Liberals, but these voluntary workers supported her on the same grounds as 90111 e ComPrratives. namely, that she was by far the mot suitable candidate. It is complained that, workers of the Conservative persuasion did not turn out very readily to support the candidates of their own way of thinking. Well, perhaps vo'nntrity work is not in their line; they like a quid pro quo. But still lam told that Mr. Pilling at all events could not. complain of fl want of hell" by Conservative ofillials. It looked very much as thougil all the party machinery had been set. in (motion on his behalf, in spite of the understanding to the contrary.

flow is this for an "election humour." A well- known Tory sent his carriage into Orford ward on Ntonday to help in bringing Mr. Shaw's supporters to th. poll, hut the driver having been wrongly instructed, or having misunderstood his in6truc- tions, commenced bringing Mr. Cross's voters and kApt, on at this good work nearly the whole of the afternoon h?fore the mistake was discovered. dr. Shaw is very unfortunate.

VOLUNTEER PRIZE DISTRIBUTION.

The annual prize distribution of the First Battalion P. W.V.South Lancashire Regiment took place on Friday night last at the Drill Hall, Major Ridgway presided and presented the prizes. There were alsi present Captain Edelston, Appleton, Chorlev, Fairclough, Silverthorne. Major RinowAv in opening the proceedings, said that he was sorry to say that toe Colonel could not be pre- sent. The battalion was never in a better state than it was now. The present strength of the ; battalion was 689 men out of a possible strength of 704, which was the strongest number since ho joined it 15 years ago With regard to the camp, it was thought, that a change was necessary. They bad been three years in brigade camp, but now it was decided to go into regimental camp, and the battalion would go on the last week in June, which was holiday week at Warrington, and therefore more convenient for the men. It had been , stated that Colonel Greenall was retiring. That ought never to have appeared, as it ought first to have been announced in the orders. Colonel Greenall was the old- est colonel in Lancashire and had done a great deal for the Volunteer movement. . _

Captain SILVEHTHORNE said that the figure of merit this year was 151.93, while last year it was 157-75. The recruits figure of merit last year was 147, while this year it showed a large increase, being 170. Major Ridgway then proceeded to distribute the prizes. PROPOSED EXTENSION OF THE BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. The CLERIC read a letter from the Local Government Board which stated that the Town Clerk of Warrington had .(ent a representation in favour of the extension of the borough boundary. They wished to have the obser- vations of the Board upon it within three week's time. The Clerk observed that he had received notice from the Town Clerk of Warrington, and he understood that it was proposed to inelude in the berough the whole of the township of Little S tnkey. all that portion of Poulton with Fearnhead, known as the Howley district, and the Cheshire townships as far as the SUip Canal. Of course the Authority had now nothing to do with the Cheshire ' townships. On the last application for extension the Authority supported the application to include Little Sankey, except Gate Wharf farm, but opposed the inclusion of Poulton—Mr. W. Houghton said that he understood that the tenant of Gate Wharfe fatm 'was not now opposed to the proposed extension, but was rather anxious for the alteration, and desired to be in the borough. He therefore proposed that the Authority support the application with regard to the Liverpool- road 'side of the borough but oppose it with regard to the Poulton in Fearnhead district.—Mr. Heaton seconded and the motion was passed unanimously.—Mr.' W. Houghton wished to point out several astonishing state- ments in the case from the Warrington Corporation. It was stated that there was no elementary school or burial ground in the township of Poulton with Fearnhead, and also that the Rural Sanitary Authority were in favour of the borough being extended in that direction.—The Clerk promised that the matter would be laid properly before the Local Government Board.

VOTES OF THANKS. Mr. W. Long proposed a vote of thanks to the Rev. G. F. Robert., the Chairman of the Authority. Mr. Roberts was unfortunately not strong in health, and it was therefore the more praiseworthy and plucky for him to stick to his post as he had clone during the past twelve months.—Mr. W. Houghton seconded and Mr. Heaton supported the motion which was passed unani- mousiv.—Mr. Heaton proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Long for his services as vice-chairman. He said that they were very sorry that owing to the changes which had taken place. Mr. Long would not be able to be with them again.—Mr. A. Jolley seconded and Mr. W. Hough- ton supported the motion which was carried.—Mr. Long briefly replied.—A vote of thanks to Mr. Balharry. as chairman of the assessment committtee was proposed by Mr. Long, seconded by Mr. W. Heaton and supported by Mr. Heaton.—Mr. Balharry replied.—A similar compli- ment was paid to the officers of Authority to which Messrs. S. Sutton, and Kerfoot, and Dr. Sephton The proceedings then terminated.

In some parts of America the work of recording votes —— at elections is done by an ingenious mechanical contri- vance. The device counts the vote as it is recorded by the voter himself. and the result is known immediately at the crosing of the poll.

THE WATCH. COMMITTEE AND- CHRISTMAS bAY ENTERTAINMENTS. Sir,—Passing through the streets the other day I was surprised to see a notice announcing a sacred c oncert to take olace onChristmas Day,in the Royal Court Theatre, Warrington. On inquiring into the matter I find that the project has been sanctioned by sbur Watch Commit- tee, in spite of the fact that charges are to be made for admission. Now, as other places of amusement have in times past been refused permission to do this kind of thing on the ground that it is illegal to give concerts in this way on sacred days, it seems very strange why the proprietors of the Royal Court Theatre have been al- lowed this privilege. Can you enlighten me as to what new circumstances have moved the Watch Committee to alter their line of procedure ?—Yours, Etc., FAIR PLAY. THE "NEW CURE" FOR DIPHTHERIA. Sir,—Dr. Dulberg, of Manchester, in a letter to a *con- temporary on the anti-toxin treatment of diphtheria, writes :—" I do not think the time has yet come when by an appeal to the public the latter is to draw the in- ference that the medical profession is unanimous in declaring the new remedy as safe and certain. I am strengthened in my opinion by the following extract from a German newspiper : Referring to the rumoured death of three children who had been inoculated with the anti-diphtheritic serum, Professor Ehrlich announced at the meeting of the German Society for Public Health that he had succeeded in tracing one such case. It re- lated to a child that had been inoculated some months ago with the serum as a protection against infection, but having lately acquired true diphtheria, died in spite of injections with Bahring's, fluid.' Professor Ehrlich is one of the pioneers of the new remedy.' And, as I opined, and said so in "Vanity Fair" and several papers, the " specific "is perilous. Or. Hansrmann, an assistant of the distinguishedrv'irchow, saYsi (" British Medical Journal," December at't, p; 1320) •" that ' serum injections could by no means be considered ha) less, as affections of the kidneys had frequently folio' in one case more severe in type than had ever yet been observed in diphtheria." The anti-toxin may also con- tain the germs of glanders, and other diseases. The Royal Commission on Vaccination, which is still sitting, is going to recommetsd, among other recommendations, I understand, for a "pure lymph "—that is, according to the ideas of the Commissioners, a lymph free from blood, as pro-vaccinal evidence on the subject goes to prove that it is owing to "bad lymph "—by which term is meant a lymph not free from blood—that vaccination is attended with risks and frequently followed by dis- ease, in many cases with a direct fatal ending. Yet this anti-toxin is a cultivation of this very dangerously ob- noxious element !—Yours faithfully, JOSEPH COLLINSON. Wolsingbam, Co. Durham, Dec. 15th, 1894.

NEW LIGHTS ON VEGETABLE PHYSIOLOGY. ' Sir,—Allow me to thank Mr. Summeri for the admirable service he is rendering to the caus& of religions liberalism. He he doing yeoman's service in exposing, however unconsciously, the follies and futili- ties of his creed. He is an excellent illustration of the fact I have insisted upon before—that faith and reason. jug won't do mix-d ; they are incompatible, and he who attempts this unnatural amalgamation is sure to lose one cf them. Many a reason has been so lost, and

_ not a few faiths. It passes my comprehension—perhaps Mr. Simmers will tell sat—where he got his botany from? It is evi- dently not of this world, so I presume it must be from another sphere. His astounding revelations in vegetable physiology are calculated to cause great emotion in scientific circles. I believe laughter is considered to be an emotion And truly, among all the dreary comicali- ties of the Christmas papers nothing so irresistibly comic has appeared as are the unhappy flounderines of Mr. Felix Summers on the vegetable physiology. It is sincerely to be wished that the Editer will not move the closure until this new Warrington worthy has emptied his scientific repertoire I. And what a versatile genius it is, which can swivel round from absolute scepticism and denial of even one germ in a wheat grain to a too generous bestowal of not one but many in the same grain! Our Mr. Summers has a splendid facility in giving himself away. Listen to this, oh ye botanists! " Let us." exclaiens this savant in a moment of inspira- tion, "let us consider for a mement what would happen if the seed did not die. Why, it would continue to throw out new plants." Shades of Hooker, Arnett, Bentley ' and Balfour,what a revelation! Would an egg" continue to throw out" new chickens? Your correspondent's acquirements in natural science, and consequently his fitness to reasonably discuss this matter, may be gathered from his analogy of the seed- -the product of a vegetable ovary—to the ovary of an animal! Alas! for the dogma of the dying of the dead, if this be the best champion it can produce. Like some other people, who ought to be better informed than him- self, Mr. Felix Summers imputes magical properties to the "ground" which it does not possess. Wheat can he grown to full fruition without any contact with " the ground." My quarrel was with Mr. Summers' "text," as I found it in a so-called scientific book, and not with the Jewish carpenter or tent-maker, whose utterances are out of the pale of scientific dispute. But when a pseudo-scientist tells me that a grain of wheat " must die are it quicken," I appeal for further information. What is it that dies? Not the living part, I am _told Avide Ben Cheyill) ; then it must be the detail part! govt. '.3.6 thy( wonder performed ? Oh! changed,you know, 14, and that's a kind of death! So! when a load of that is ' turned—that's changed too—but I have yet to be told that the coal dies in the process. But we should have ' bad people silly enough to maintain this if some Jewish artizan had said so. To the Buddhist the pagoda has no shadow; and Christianity has its shadowless pagodas, every bit as unreasonably maintained. Something of excuse must he extended to our local illuminator for his over hasty judgments upon a ques- Mina so utterly beyond him when we consider the dis- may he must experience—as one of the "blind leaders of the blind "—now he finds he has been for so lone dangling this physinal misstatement before the eyes of his flock as a divine revelation. No man will pest- attain to beavers by believing an untruth—much leas se by tp,iehing it, knowing it to b. false. In very truth this "riving of the dead cinema was a Paean idea, borrowed to act as a foundation for the absurd and impossible do2m-t of trelily resurrection. Your readers will note that tin se arguments by no means militate against the idea of the immortality of the spirit. Indeed I indulge the hope of meetieg friend Summers in the "land of the leal." Prrbaos there, " where the wicked cease from troubling and the 'wears are at rest," even Mr. Summers and I may he able to see more nearly eye to eye and agree to differ liBs. So [nought it be.— Truly yours, JOHN PEEru4.'

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

EARLESTO%N BAPTIST CHURCH CHOIR. On Wednesday evening the members of the abQve choir with a number of friends, had a social evening in the school-room. An excellent supper was partaken of,after which the evening was spent in singing and games. ST. JOHN'S MISSION HALL. A Christmas tree and sale of work was held in the above hall, on Saturday evening. There was a variety of articles for disposal. of a useful and ornamental character, and as there was a good attendance of the public, a fairly good sale was made. During the even- ing an entertainment was given by the Amateur Minstrel troupe. The sale was continued on Monday evening, when there was again a p,ood attendance, and a fair business was done. The proceeds were in aid of the Mission Hall fund.

CHRISTMAS TIDE AT EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

The preparations for Christmas, were if anything on a More extensive scale than previously, and the shop- keepers vied with each other to make their windows as attractive as it was possible to do so. On Christmas Eve the streets presented an enlivening aspect, and until past midnight large numbers of people were about. The Viaduct Works brass band turned out about seven o'clock and visited various places in the neighbourhood, where they played selections of suitable music. A new feature noticeable in the streets, was a troupe of youth- ful minstrels, who visited various places to give selec- tions of negro songs. There could also h. seen a num- ber of young fellows attired in cut-away coats, knee breeches and top hat, and wearing eigantic false noses, but their object of so appearing Was best known to them- Christmas morning opened in a disheartenin er, rain -commencing to 'fill early, on, arsd thi ito make the fireside gatherings more numerous they otherwise would have been. Services were at the various churches in. the district, but wet- were only meagre. A fair number of feet,- enth asts braved the elements, and spent a cruple if hours witnessing a match .on the Earlestown football ground. Business at the Post Offices were very brisk, and at the railway stations the bookings were fairly heavy. All the works in the neighbourhood, with the exception of Meesre. McCorquodale's, were closed until Thursday morning, and all the tradespeople cl shops on Wednesday. , .• THE OLD YEAR. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

Warringtpn ha i every reason to be satisfied with al( I! I Christata.i matches, for on all three occasions they came out on the right side, scoring a total of 31 points to 8. This is a very good record, and there is one circum- stance which makes it all the better, and that is that in two of the matches, Stockport and T) Idesley, Warring- ton took the opportunity of having sweet revenge. The Warrington team have not had a very good time of it during the first half of the season,which closes on Mon- day, but they have finished it in capital style.

The gale which worked such havoc all over the king- dom, had a try at the football ground, but could do nothing worse than, blow down a quantity of boards which are placed round the field. It it could do noth- ing very serious to the palisading or grand stand, it, however, completely spoiled the Stockport match, for when the ball simply bounced off the ground, it was at once conveyed to the next field by the wind. With such things as these happening it can well be imagined that real football was out of the question altogether.

It was very evident on Saturday however that War- rington were the better team, for whereas Stockpert very rarely got away against the wind, Warrington had several prolonged stays at the half-way line and even beyond under similar circumstances. Nina tenths of the game wore played in one corner of the field, and the attacking players seemed to have a special liking for that corner. limo after time the defenders would send them back only to see them come again.

With such a game it is not very fair to single out players for comparison. Each side defended well as it is shown by the low score of a try to nil, and both War- rington and Stockport are to be congratulated on sherw- ing such pluck in facing the tremendous gale. Barber was the scorer of the only try of the match which gave Warrington the victory.

For Christmas Day the fare was fyldeslev who had inflicted three weeks ago the only defeat Warrington had received at home this season. Warrington were assisted by Tom Garritty, of Broughton Rangers, who was on a visit to his native town. The match was a very well contested one all through, and Warrington were able to beat the " Bongers by a penalty goal and two tries to a goal. What a pity this was not a league match Warrington must keep up their form, and then they will stand a chance of beating Tyldesley away from home, and thus capture the two points which they lost at Warrington. A noticeable feature of the match on Tuesday was that both tries were scored by the same forward, Houghton.

Still another match ! Surely three matches in five days is plenty for any player. The West Hartlepcof team, who are at present on tour, visited Warrington on Wednesday, and Warrington took the opportunity of gaining their biggest victory of the present season, win- ning by twenty points to three. The home forwards were much too good for the Durham team, and as a rr- sult the three-quarters were:able to do more than defend. They were net slow to show what they can do, when they go t half a chance, and Burton scored twice, and Heesom and Barber once each.

Although iVarrington piled on so heavy a score they had not by any means things all their own way. In a great measure the superior play of the home forwards as a lot, was the pivot round which Warrineton's repeated successos,clustered. Nevins was as active as a hare and as strong as a horse. Turner toe was generally in the thick of it but the others to a man played well, and I repeat that it was Warrington's superiority in this de- partment that laid the foundation of their success.

With two such half-backs as Smack Bate and Tom Garritty (of Broughton Rangers fame) the advantage was still further improved upon. The manner in which these two watched the scrimmagers and fed their three- varters was a treat to witness.

The three-quarter line almost monopolised the atten- tion of the onlookers. The two wings Barber and Bur- ton undoubtedly came in for chief honours, their passes, short and long, seemed to be effected with magnetic at- traction, so precise in delivery and accurate in receiving were they all. Acid to this their speediness and unselfish play and it will he seen they were the right men in the right place. Haesom was sure if not brilliant, and Moores astonished ua all by one or two famous tackles. There has been a breath of suspicion that Moores was " afeared " to tackle, but the manner in which he single- handed, successfully tackled the burly Gibbons was in itself 8 ufficient t) dispel any such croakings.

--- As to 13,42c0w he was safe and strong as ever and after the win was assurAd became venturesome inte the bar- gain. It showed well but under different circumstances might have proved risky. The game itself was free and open, and pleasantly devoid of any unnecessary rough-

MEETING OF THE RELIEF COMMITTEE

Another meeting of the Relief Committee was held at the Town Hall, on Friday evening, under the presidency of Mr. Taylor, JR. There were also present : The Revs. Canon Wood, J. Simpson, and J. Ftolden ; Dr. McDougall, Dr. Ormsby, Messrs. F. J. Norman, J.P., P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., E. Irnison, M. Sweetinan, W. M. Williams, S. Pope, J. Poole, R. H. Posnett, A. R. Norman, and J. W. Lightburn.—The report of the sub- committee was submitted. It showed that they recom- mended that the various wards be the area for the dis- tribution of relief. That a committee be formed for each ward, consisting of the councillors and guardians. with power to add to their number, the clergy to attach themselves to the division in which they consider their services wou'd be ntost useful. That the committee arrange for the place for meeting, preference being given to the polling booths ; and that they obtain a copy of the ward register. That they notify the public that on a specified night they would attend to receive applica- tions for relief, such to be made in person or by letter. That as far as possible they refer the applicant to their last employer. That they refer chronic cases of poverty or persons in receipt of parish pay and those not tempor- arily out of employntent, to the poor law authority. That cases net included in the last two sentences be investigated by the ward committee, scheduled, and presented by the chairman of each ward to the weekly meeting of the General Committee. That a Supervision Committee, consisting of Canon Wood, Dr. McDougall, and Mr. Taylor, be appointed to see to the constitution and working of the ward committees, and that they meet an hour before the General Coen- naittee to receive, examine, and confirm the recommen- dations of the chairmen of the wards and the employers of labour. That the General Committee meet at eight o'clock each Thursday evening to receive the reports of the Finance and Supervising Committees, determine the amount of relief to be granted, and to distribute the orners.—The 'report was adopted.—lt was decided to elect a Finance Committee,conesting of Mr. Taylor,the , Rev. J. Holden, Messrs. F. J. Norman, J.P., W. Collier, E. Imison, and Callender. It was reported that Messrs. Walker and Sons, of War- rington, had promised to give L 25 to the Relief Fund, and that Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co.lhad decided to give £2o.—lt was agreed that the ward committees should meat on the following evening (Saturday), to re- ceive applications. This concluded the business of the meeting.—The ward committees met on Saturday even- ng and their recom nendations were submitted to the General Committee on Monday evening, when a large number of orders were distributed. On Monday evening the first meeting of the supervis- ing committee for the distribution of the relief in con- nection with the unernployed fund at Runcorn, took place at the Town Hall,under the presidency of the Rev. Canon Wood. The managers of the works of the United Alkali Co., at Runcorn and Widnes, presented reports of families, resident in Runcorn, deserving assistance. Lists were also submitted by the Ward 'Committees. Great pains were taken to see that no names were dupli- cated. In no cases of chronic poverty was relief given. Orders for food were given to deserving applicants. The General Committee met again on Thursday evening to go fully into these subjects.

FRODSHA.M.

PAIZOCIIIAL CoarßorrEn.—Tho Frodsharn Parochia, Committee met in the Town Hall on Saturday, Mr. A. Thomas in the chair. The waterworks sub-committee submitted their report which was adopted, with the exception of a clause relating to some improvement to the engine house. This was referred back to the sub- committee for further consideration.—On the motion of Mr. H. G. Linaker, seconded by Mr. Williams. it was resolved that the best thanks of the committee be accorded to Mr. Thomas for his services as chairman.— A meeting of the committee was also held on the 26th inst., when Mr. Samuel Davies, the superintending engineer of the waterworks, explained that he had nearly completed the work in respect of which he had been appointed, and expressed his willingness, as the committee was going out of office, to resign at once without the fortnight's notice to which he was entitled. It was resolved that inasmuch as all the work had not been completed, his resignation should be accepted as from the end of 14 days from the 29th inst., and that it should be left with the new committee to re-engage his services if they should think it necessary so to de.—The minutes were passed. The rev. gentleman, who is leaving the district, hasi laboured energetically in the parish for five years. " ROPING " A WEDDING PARTY AT STOCKTON HEATH.

ACTION AT THE WARRINGTON COUNTY COURT. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday last,be- fore his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes,William 808 ock, builder, Stockton Heath, sued the Warrington Carriage Company for damages to a stone coping in Stockton Heath, to the amount of 12s. Mr. A. Browne appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. P. Davies defended. Mr. Browne said that the claim was only a small one but it affected the Vlarrington Carriage Company to a large _ extent for other similar cases might happen. The amount claimel was for damages to a coping stone through the negligence of one of the cabmen of the Company. There was a custom in the Warrington district, and had been for years, which was called " roping a wedding party," which meant that two parties held a rope across • the mal and thus prevented the wedding party from going home from church. On the present occasi,m, the party had been " roped "four times on their way home, and the last timo the rope was tied to some palisading in front of one of Mr. Bostock's houses, two women holding the other end. The cabman on coming up, saw it, btr; did nut stop the horse, and the result was that when it forced its way past the rope, the palisading was pulled down, and damage done to the coping stone to the amount of 12s. It might be asked, how were the Carriage Company liable ? He submitted that if the driver, when driving alonc, the highway, did not use ordinary care, and did damage to any person, he was responsible for the damage. He understood that the facts of the case were not disputed. His Honour: He was exercising his right of way. Re might break a gate if it were in his way. I think it is a pity that the old revered custom referred to, should be the subject of litigation. Many of the old customs are not to be deprecated. Mr. Browne: But property should not be injured. Mr. Davies said that he almost agreed with Mr. Browne in everything except that the driver stopped the vehicle, and asked the women who were holding the rope to take it away. He had already been stopped three times, and this time he determined to pass, and put his horse abreast of the rope, in ignorance that the other end was fastened to the pali,ade, as a number of women were there also. We submitted that if the rope had pulled Mr. Bostock's house down, the driver was not responsible. The affair took place in Red-lane. Mr. Browne: The greatest place for roping was formally the Parish Church, I have seen many a one roprd in my younger days. The Judge said that as the cab could not _get round, the cabman had the right to go through. He was only exercising his right of road. Mr. Browne said that after what the Judge had said, he could not go on with the case, and would consent to withdraw if costs were not claimed. ' Mr. Davies said that he thought it was hard on his clients as they were not liable. If:their house had been injured at the time,they could not have claimed damages from Mr. Bostock, and therefore he ought not be able to claim damages from the Company. After further discussion, the case was withdrawn by leave, Mr. Bostock to pay the costs of the cabnran, and a witness from Runcorn.

SILVER-ST. WESLEYAN DAY SCHOOL. ANNUAL CONCERT. The children's annual concert and prize distribution in with Silver-st. Wesleyan Day School was held in the Bold- st. Wesleyan Schoolroom on Thursday evening last week There was a very good attendance, and the chair was occupied by the Mayor (Councillor James Fairclough). His Worship briefly expressed the pleasure it gave him to be present and at once proceeded with the pro- gramme. The programme of the evening consisted of songs, choruses, action songs, hoop, hat, and scarf drills, and recitations, all of which were performed in a I very creditable manner under the conduCtorship of Mr. Upson, the schoolmaster, and accompanied by Miss Kelly. The scholars who gave the F(AOS and recitations were Miss Maggie Taylor, Miss Alice Hemmings, and Miss Ruth Price. The proceeds of the csncert will be devoted to the prize fund, and a Christmas tree for the , children. During an interval, the Secretary of the school, Mr. E. Guest, J.P., gave the annual report. He said that the lichool was founded almost eighteen years ago, and, considering the many drawbacks, he had every reason to congratulate the master and his assistants for the results achieved. Since the present master had entered upon his duties. nine years ago, the government grant had increased 100 per cent. The working of the schools during the first nine years cost £398 1./.s. ad., and during the last nine years £289 lls. 9d. He was sorry to say that the balance against the treasury at present was £52 19s. 7d. The premises had been twice enlarged since their erection, but they needed further enlargement for infant accommodation, large numbers having had to be turned away for lack of room. Since the school had opened. 2,750 children had passed through it. There were 301 on the books, and the average attendance for the past year had been 260, with 20 half-timers. Dur- ing the year the school had been opened 430 times, and , 97 had made over 400 attendances, and would receive I prizes. Fifteen had attended every time it was pcssible to attend. After the evening's programme had been gone through the Mayoress presented the prizes to those who had made over 400 attendances, and at the close a hearty vote of thanks was passsd to the Mayor and M tyoress for their presence, en the motion of Councillor Shaw-Green, seconded by Mr. R. G. Henshall and supported by Mr. J. W. Hayward, J.P.

CONCERT AT SANKEY BRIDGES. On Saturday evening, a grand sacred concert was held at the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Chapel, when selections were given from "The Messiah." The concert was of a first-class order, and there was a band and chorus of over sixty performers under the conductorship of Mr. W. Brough, the organist being Mr. T. Brough. It is a great pity that with such an excellent programme there was not a crowded audience, but the night was so boisterous that it militated against the success the concert, and there was only a moderate attendance, but those who braved the elements had a musical treat, for all the pieces were gone through with masterly precision, and, with the exception of the poor num- ber pre.ent,everything went off with great success. The concert opened with the overture at the com- mencement of "Messiah," which WAS cleverly per- formed by the band. Mr. L. Richardson next sang "Comfort ye," with fine feeling, and the chorus followed with the well-known piece, "The Glory of the Lord." Mr. D. Fanshaw was heard to advan- tage in " Tbus saith the Lord," and "But who shall abide?' Miss F. Bodle of London, received loud applause for her rendering of "co Thou that telleat," as also did Mr. D. Fanshaw, in "The people that walked in darkness." The chorus then sang with much force and in excellent style, " For unto us a child is born," followed by " Glory to God." "He shall feed His flock," was rendered in a manner which charmed the audier first part was taken by Miss F. and by 111 i. Bennett,the chorm in irt. "1 Id the Lamb of God," aext the cl 3, followed by "He 3if it as generally conced' 111: to the greatest ad a very successful in " All ye like sheep.' and Mr. L. Richardson was heartily applauded for the solo. "Thou didst not leave." " Lift up your heads" was sung in a rousing manner by the chorus, and the two solos," Why do the nations r ,bv Mr. Fan- shaw and "Then shalt break them," by Mr. Levi Richardson were well appreciated. The well-known "Hallelujah chorus" WAS well performed. Miss Gunton sang "I know that my Redeemer liveth "in excellent style. Mr. Fanshaw was greazly approved by the solo. "The trumpet shall sound," in which Mr. Armstrong ably accompanied on the cornet. The programme was brought to a close by the chorus, " Worthy is the lamb." The various soloists were in first, class form, and performed their du' without the slightest hitch. The choruses were sung with great power and harmony, and won the approval of the most fastidious. It was a matter of regret that Mr. A. B. Bailey, was unable to take part owing to indisposition.

:MITTEN. OA this committee were submitted at the meeting of the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority on Thursday morn- ing. The meeting of the committee was held on the 21st inst. at the Bridge Inn. Stockton Heath. Mr. Eyes I occupied the chair, and there were also present : Messrs. ' Davenport, Shackleton, Col-muck, Darhyshire, Longland, Westbrook, Whitlow, and E. J. Hall; Mr. Farrington (nuisance inspector), and Mr. Ashton (clerk). rhe Clerk read a letter from Mr. J. Longland, solicitor, Union-street, Warrington, who stated that he had been I informed the Ship Canal Company were stopping up the communication between the old portion of the river and the new river at the Warrington end. The whole por- tion of the river into which their authority had an outfall for their sewers, would again be- come merely a huge cesspool, and there would be a repetition of the nuisance which existed a few months ago. He hastened to draw their attention to the state of affairs in the hope that they might be able to do some- thing to prevent what would otherwise render a portion of their district absolutely unbearable and unfit for any- one to live in —The Clerk reported that he had com- municated with Mr. A. M. Dunlop, of the Manchester Ship Canal Company, who bad replied stating that the matter referred to was already under discussion between the Canal Company and the Warrington Corporation. It was resolved that the Clerk communicate with the Town Clerk of Warrington on the subject and if on receiving his reply he should think it necessary to do so, to press the 'Nlanchester Ship Canal Company to remedy the nuisance which their actien would cause.—Votes of thanks were passed to the Chairman of the Committee (Mr. Thomas Hall) and also to Mr. Ashton (clerk).—The Authority passed the minutes unanimously. 4110. GENERAL NEWS. tw

11.0,

Destructive floods have occurred in many parts of Holland. Whales are supposed to live anywhere from three hundred to four hundred years. Th 9 match between Stoddart's Eleven and an Eighteen of Sydriey Junior; ended in a draw. About midnight on Christmas Eve,Edmund Kesteven, a professional cricketer living in Sutton-in-Ashfield. Notts, murdered SArah Oldhatn, a married woman with whom he had been by cutting her throat. Kest- even, on being arrested, admitted having committed the,crime. The first metal bridge in England was a cast-iron structure, built in 1777 over the Severn. The cast-iron bridge across the Wear at Sunderland was opened is 1796. It is stated that the Board of Trade have, as the result of inquiries, recommended that the passenger driven and firemen on the Scottish railways should have a running day of about 150 miles, to be restricted to ten hours. The new arrangements are not viewed with favour by the men, and the employes of the Caledonian Railway have petitioned to be allowed to work on the old g ul ar:io as. A Tamatave telegram of Saturday reports the com- mencement of fighttng between the French forces and the Hava4. A Hova outpost is said to have fired on a French cruiser, which returned the fire. The 'lovas had three men killed and the French one. wounded. Captain Dreyfus, the French officer charged with !lav- ing furt.ished the agents of a foreign Power with secret daetiments enabling it to enter into hostilities against France, was convicted on Siturclay by court-martial, and was senten...-ed to deportation and internment for life in a fortified place. The employment of whisky as an antidote in case of snake-bite seems to be nearing its end. Experiments have teen tried with strychnine in cases of this sort,ano the results are highly satisfactory. It is said to remove all unpleasant symptoms, not only of snake-bite. but of mushronm poison. en the principle of fighting fire with tire, the use of this poison should be a pronoeneed SUCCe3II.

A WARRINGTON PROPERTY DISPUTE

In the Chancery Court aiverpool yesterday, Vice- Chancellor Robinson gave judgment in a case brought by Mr. Frederick John Carlisle against Messrs. W. A. Bishop and Co. The plaintiff since. 1869 had been the owner of some cettages in Dial Court, Warrington, and in recent years the defendants had acquired some adjoining property, building borax works there. The plaintiff stated that he had been in the habit of going upon the defendant's land at the back of his property, in order to make such repairs as were necessary. He claimed that continuous use had given him a right of the way which the defendants now obstructed. There was also a number of other points. The plain. ti asked for an injunction to restrain the defendants from committing such trespasses and obstruction as he alleged, and he asked that his property should be put in its original position The vice-Chancellor in giving judgment said he thcught it was desirable that he should state the grounds upon which he refused to grant a mandatory injunction,aed why he had not called upon Mr. Hughes, counsel for the defendants, on that part of the case. In this case the injustice of a mandatory order would be even greater than it might be in some cases. The small cot, tage property, of which the plaintiff was the owner- was liable to be condemned by the Corporation of War- rington at some early date. On the question of the obstruction of Ugh of three cottages he decided in favou of the plaintiff, and he granted £5O in regard to that part of the case. He directed that certain conveniences which were complained of should be removed, and he awarded £25 in respect of some lights. As to the plain- tiff's eaves and gutters being damaged by the defendant's works he had arrived at the conclusion that there was damage at No. 24, and that a proper gutter should be put up. For that he awarded £25, which would include any loss which the plaintiff or his tenant had sustained by dampness at No. 21. In reference t the claim to go on defendant's land to do repairs, the continuous user which was necessary had not been esta- blished, and he decided in the defendants' favour. He also decided for the defendants in regard to certain brickwork, holding that it was under-pinning to support the plaintiff's building. He thought the plaintiff was entitled to a declaration that he had a right to drain from the roofs of his houses into Flag-yard.—Mr. Hughes objected to this, and eventually his Honour decided not to make the declaration.

AN ENGLISHMAN'S HOUSE IS HIS CASTLE or, properly speaking, ought to be. 'This can never be the case, if unfortunately a would-be householder has to resort to the Easy Payment System, a new name for the int. quitous Hire System. To furnish a house on the Hire System, alias the Easy Payment System, is to establish and support what is the most cruel, arbitrary extortion ever invented. The prices charged for the goods are enormous, without any regard for quality, the agreements are one-sided, being drawn with a cuteness .which would do credit to a money lender, full of despotic and arbitrary restrictions, suchas not to remove the mark on the furniture, not to remove the furniture, not to wear the furniture. At all times to allow the mercenaries to inspect the furniture. To pay the instal- ments regularly, and if oneis missed to forfeit the furniture and all the moneys you may have paid, that is to say, although you might have paid £l9 on £23 worth of furniture, but missed the last payment by a single day, the vultures could empty our house and stick to the £l9 paid, as well as the furniture. Other conditions such as permission to break open forcibly night or day, the outer and inner doors of your house, in order to take away the goods, and above all, that no matter how badly and wickedly they may treat you that you undertake not to resort to legal proceedings. Be warned in time, have nothing to do with easy payment or hire system. If you require assistance go to Mr. W. H. Worsley, " The Pantheon," Gaythorne, Manchester, the largest House Fur- nisher in the North, who will treat you y.ith consideration, sell you goods at the lowest cash price, and, if required, allow you time to pay for them, charging you 10 per cent over invoice price for the convenience. For instance Mr. Worsley has several HOUSES OF FUR- NITURE on view at £l5 155., £2O, £25, £3O. £52, £lOO, all at the lowest cash price, and not a penny less. Any of the above suppl.vd subject to credit, upon 10 per cent being added to the amount. Particulars post free.
